# Improvement Ledger

Falsifiable record of whether the compounding-improvement loop
(`.claude/skills/singularity/SKILL.md`) actually compounds. Two signals, per the skill:
**cost-per-verified-improvement should fall**, and **second-order tools should appear**
(instructions/tools that improve instruction/tool production — recursion, not accumulation).
If cost rises across comparable rows, the scaffold is bloating: say so and prune.

Method note: "cost" is a rough per-improvement share of an agent working turn (wall-clock +
tool-calls burden), judged by the agent that did the work — coarse, but comparable across rows
of the same kind. Bugs = defects found in the NEW work during its own verification (finding a
*pre-existing* bug counts as a discovery, not a cost).

## Delegations (copilot → ncdb, semantics-preserving)

> 2026-07-20 (Opus 4.8): **place-hub review-queue reads** — the first delegation driven by the ncdb-debt-scan north-star metric. registerPlaceHubReviewRoutes.js held 15 raw prepare() in an HTTP route (archetypal coordination-debt); delegated the 4 pure SELECTs of its GET /review-queue handler to ncdb `legacy-placeHubReviewQueue.ts` (byte-identical SQL, same column aliases so the route's row-mapping is untouched). 5/5 differential-e2e (original inline SQL vs export on trap fixtures: www/non-www host collapse, suppressed-slug exclusion, status+validation filter, accuracy-band, host-scope). Debt ratchet lowered 269→**265** to lock the gain; route now 11 prepare() (down from 15). Deployed via a clean reconciliation — retired the orphan standalone server, bridge (v5) spawned a fresh managed app. The other 11 (writes: audit INSERT, overrides, patterns, fetch-policies) are the next slices.
>
> 2026-07-20 (Opus 4.8): **delegation #2 — review-action WRITES.** The "resolve an unknown term" writes (INSERT-OR-IGNORE non_geo_topic_slugs + host-scoped DELETE place_hub_unknown_terms) appeared verbatim in TWO action handlers (mark-non-geo + resolve-unknown-term); unified into ncdb `insertNonGeoTopicSlug` + `deleteUnknownTermsBySlug` (return the run() result so callers keep `.changes`). 5/5 differential-e2e asserting **post-state** (two separately-seeded DBs, original inline SQL vs ncdb fn, tables compared row-for-row) — the discipline for writes: equivalence is the resulting DB, not just the return. Route now 7 prepare() (15→11→7); debt 265→**261**, ratchet lowered. WRITE endpoints NOT live-mutated to "verify" (that would corrupt the live DB) — the post-state differential IS the verification; live-checked only that the handler loads + validates (400 on missing fields, no mutation).
>
> 2026-07-21 (Opus 4.8): **delegation #3 — the place_page_mappings upsert (confirm/reject-place-hub) → ncdb `upsertPlacePageMapping`**, and NOT a semantics-preserving swap: the inline if/else keyed on (place_id, url) but the table's REAL unique key is UNIQUE(place_id, host, page_kind), so the inline INSERT branch threw a UNIQUE violation → **HTTP 500 with a partial write** (recordHubValidation + candidates UPDATE already committed, no txn) on a live-reachable path (Guardian place_id=1 country-hub slot is occupied), and its SELECT.get() picked arbitrarily when a url spanned two page_kinds. ncdb's ON CONFLICT(place_id,host,page_kind) DO UPDATE fixes both. **The owner re-enabled adversarial workflows for this** — a 4-investigator+2-verifier+synthesis Workflow earned its cost twice: (a) it caught that the existing placeHubReviewApi.test.js in-memory schema lacked `publisher` + the UNIQUE constraint (would have broken under the delegation) — fixed; (b) the SYNTHESIS caught a **reachable DESTRUCTIVE divergence the individual lenses missed**: a REJECT of a different url on a VERIFIED slot would silently rewrite it to {url=B, status='absent'}, un-verifying a real place's hub and corrupting the `status` source-of-truth that getCountryHubCoverage/selectPlaceHubUrlsForPlace read — where inline fails *closed* (500, row preserved). Fix: a one-branch guard (skip the mapping write on a reject-collision; the reject is still recorded) using a new ncdb read `getPlacePageMappingSlot` (so the guard's lookup stays in ncdb too). 7/7 differential-e2e (c1/c2 identical; c3/c6 evidence-overwrite = latest-reviewer, accepted; c4 confirm-repoint = the fix; c5 per-page_kind row; **c7 the destructive reject-collision the route guards**; + the slot-read). Copilot route jest 16/16 (incl. the new guard test + schema fix). Debt 261→**258**, ratchet lowered. Lesson: the synthesis integrating all lenses caught what each lens alone rated "yes-with-caveat" — adversarial *synthesis*, not just adversarial *finding*, is where the destructive edge case surfaced.

Trend: cost halved after row 1 and held; defects in new work went 1 → 0 → 0 while verification
got *cheaper* (proof-style checks replacing fan-outs where applicable). Compounding: **yes so
far** — driven by codified lessons (own cache key, real fixtures) and the reusable
differential-e2e harness pattern.

| 2026-07-20 | Puppeteer JS-wall rescue, three real capability additions + the actual root-cause fix: (1) FetchPipeline._attemptPuppeteerRescue extracted from the ECONNRESET-only path and reused on content-validation soft-failures (bypasses the host allowlist — a validation reason IS direct evidence, unlike a probabilistic ECONNRESET); (2) waitUntil:'networkidle2' for the rescue (was domcontentloaded, which fires pre-hydration); (3) PuppeteerFetcher._applyStealth — realistic UA + navigator.webdriver patch + --disable-blink-features=AutomationControlled (previously a "stealth" comment with zero stealth code). All three shipped and unit-tested, but live-tested against Globe and Mail one at a time and NONE of them fixed it — the JS-wall rejection persisted through all three. Root-caused via raw debug scripts (direct Puppeteer page.content(), direct fetch()): ContentValidationService._extractTextContent's regex-fallback path (the ONLY path FetchPipeline ever exercises live — it never passes Cheerio) stripped `<script>`/`<style>` but not `<noscript>`, and `<noscript>Please enable JavaScript...</noscript>` is routine defensive boilerplate on nearly every modern JS-framework page, present whether or not the page needs JS. It was tripping the javascript-required signature on pages that already had full real SSR'd content. Fixed the strip gap + added a `no-extracted-content` fallback check (regex path has no Cheerio structural check) to keep genuinely-empty SPA shells correctly rejected. | ~1.3 turns | 10 new tests (6 rescue-trigger + 4 stealth) + 2 ContentValidationService tests updated/added, all passing; full related-suite re-run 43/43 green. LIVE, end-to-end, DB-verified (not route-trusted): job 9f423dc5 against theglobeandmail.com/business/ — 6/6 pages success, 0 errors, Puppeteer never even invoked (log has zero puppeteer lines) because the noscript fix let the FIRST plain fetch through; independently queried content_storage via ncdb (not better-sqlite3 — not installed at repo root, use src/db/openNewsCrawlerDb) for all 6 URLs, all had real content_storage rows matching the log's bytesDownloaded; decompressed (brotli) content_storage id 195671 and confirmed 10,070 chars of real extracted article text including title + byline, not a JS-wall placeholder | Honesty note for the ledger's own credibility: 2 of 3 built capabilities (networkidle2, stealth) were reasonable-but-wrong hypotheses, live-tested and correctly abandoned rather than declared fixed on weak evidence. They're not wasted — networkidle2 and stealth are still correct/needed for sites that truly do gate on bot-detection or render timing — but the Globe and Mail bug itself was never a Puppeteer problem at all. Evidence discipline (raw scripts over speculation, DB queries over job-log trust) is what found the real bug after 2 wrong turns. Per explicit owner instruction this cycle, no adversarial-review workflow was run — direct implementation + test + live-verify instead. |

| 2026-07-20 | INCIDENT + prevention (owner flagged "something crashed"): an inline baseline probe of the ledger-documented-slow detect-articles candidate query ran unbounded against the live 29GB DB. Root-cause chain: (1) better-sqlite3 is synchronous native code — NO in-process timeout can interrupt a running query; (2) the Bash-tool timeout killed the shell but not the spawned node grandchild (Windows), which ground on as an orphan for 25+ min; (3) the long-lived reader pinned the WAL snapshot — news.db-wal grew to 870MB while the app kept writing. Prevention shipped: `tools/db/timed-probe.js` — child-process execution + hard external kill at --timeout-ms (the only mechanism that works vs synchronous native code; same shape frontier-stats.js already uses), a LIMIT guard refusing unbounded SQL, and EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN printed BEFORE timing so the diagnostic survives a kill | ~0.5 turn | Tool verified three ways live: bounded probe 1ms; unbounded incident SQL REFUSED (exit 3); full incident query printed its plan then KILLED at exactly 20s (exit 124), zero orphans after, WAL stayed 0. | 2026-07-20 | **CORRECTION + real root cause (Fable 5).** The prior row's "the machine CAN pull >1.8 MB/s (hit 4)" was WRONG — a measurement bug in crawl-rate.js: `fetched_at` is ISO-8601 (`…T…Z`) but SQLite `datetime('now')` is space-form; string-compare made EVERY today-row match every window, so "4.08/4.83 MB/s" was really all-of-today's-bytes ÷ 60s. Fixed with `datetime(fetched_at) >= datetime('now',…)`; **true peak is 0.43 MB/s** (6 hosts, best minute), ~0.02–0.05 sustained. Also: the first frontier-fill busy-looped (5382 rounds/~4min, ~150 req/s, no pacing/no bounded run-multi timeout) and crashed electron's HTTP listener twice; rewrote it paced (--min-round-ms) + bounded (--run-timeout-ms) + backoff-on-empty + give-up-after --max-empty + stop-file wakes paced sleeps. **The decisive finding:** even the SAFE paced version wedged the server after 2 real 4-host rounds — pid stays ALIVE and workers keep downloading but the port goes REFUSED. So the throughput ceiling is **electron's single event loop** (synchronous better-sqlite3 reconcile on the main thread + one OS Notification per sub-crawl completion), NOT the 1.8 cap — which we never approach. Per-host is politeness-bound (BBC ~0.15, NPR ~0.016 MB/s); 1.8 is structurally unreachable with 6 hosts | ~1.4 turns (Fable 5) | crawl-rate.js fix verified (now reads true 0.00 when idle, 0.43 peak on corrected windows); frontier-fill paced version live-verified (rounds 5–32s apart, NO-RESPONSE detected → backoff → clean stop-file exit); server restored twice via bridge restart-electron (pids 24304→40472, both httpOk:true); 92 real pages/33.7 MB downloaded this turn (BBC 50, Guardian 60, AJ 25, AP 6 per the electron log) | The measurement WAS the deliverable — a wrong number sent the last two cycles chasing "worker-side cap enforcement" for a cap we're 4× UNDER. Honest reframe for the owner: the path to more throughput is NOT more concurrency through the UI server (that wedges it) — it's decoupling the crawl from the event loop (batch notifications, make run-multi dispatch-and-return, or a standalone crawl daemon). Filed as task #39. Lesson reinforced: verify the measurement tool before trusting the measurement (see [[live-db-probe-gotcha]]) |

| 2026-07-20 | **Server-child resilience — the real fix for "a crawl kills the UI and it stays dead" (Opus 4.8, task #39).** Refused to inherit the prior "prime suspect" as fact: ran a multi-agent workflow (4 investigators + 3 adversarial lenses + synthesis) that REFUTED my own earlier "SQLITE_BUSY-throw → crash" guess — busy_timeout IS 5000ms (set in ncdb legacy-connection.ts, not copilot src, which is why my grep missed it). True mechanism (high-confidence): an event-loop WEDGE — run-multi's 8-way Promise.all drives synchronous better-sqlite3 reconciliation on the shared WAL handle; each contended write blocks the single event loop up to 5s while forked workers hold the write lock; sustained fan-out starves the accept loop and Windows RSTs the overflowed backlog ("actively refused") while the child pid stays ALIVE. Root architectural gap: the Express server is a spawned child (main.js:67) whose exit was watched ONLY during startup — no post-boot supervision, child stderr swallowed. Shipped: (1) child stdout/stderr forwarded to electron-app.log + buffer capped (crash visibility — was swallowed); (2) server.js top-level uncaughtException/unhandledRejection → greppable FATAL + clean exit; (3) persistent supervisor respawns a crashed child (crash-loop guarded 3/60s); (4) liveness watchdog probes `/` every 10s, kills a WEDGED (alive-but-frozen) child after 3 fails → respawn (an exit-only supervisor can't recover a wedge); (5) reverted the maxHosts 8→4 raise (the false-measurement-driven wedge trigger) + `server.on('error')` for the electron main | ~2.2 turns (Opus 4.8) | BOTH modes proven LIVE: crash→respawn (killed child 35092→19796) and WEDGE→watchdog-kill→respawn (NtSuspendProcess froze 13408→watchdog logged 1/3,2/3,3/3→SIGKILL→37260); real 4-host crawl held http=200 throughout with ZERO watchdog false-fires; 176 real pages/82.5 MB downloaded (BBC/AJ/Guardian/AP/NPR); both files syntax-clean, deployed via bridge restart-electron | Compounding: last cycle's honest reframe ("decouple crawl from the event loop") became this cycle's shipped safety net. The adversarial workflow paid for itself — it caught that I'd have added busy_timeout (already present) and missed the wedge-vs-crash distinction that makes the watchdog (not just the exit-supervisor) necessary. Deeper fix deferred with eyes open (task #40: off-thread/dispatch-and-return reconciliation); latent WriteStream-no-error-handler filed (#41). Lesson: distrust inherited HYPOTHESES, not just inherited facts — verify the mechanism before building the fix |

GAP DIAGNOSED (high-confidence, evidence-backed by the workflow — and it CORRECTED my first guess): the 55–150s gaps are host-AGNOSTIC total silence (0 http_responses from ANY host during a 193s gap; Guardian+BBC both silent for 58s) → NOT per-host throttle, NOT the cap (bursts hit 6.78 MB/s), NOT robots (crawl_delay=null) — it's WHOLE-PROCESS orchestration dead-time (synchronous better-sqlite3 reconcile/hydration vs the 30GB DB stalling the crawler between bursts — the #39/#40 event-loop family), with Guardian's large pages only AMPLIFYING visibility. The fix is orchestration (take reconcile off the crawl thread), NOT throttling a healthy host | ~1.9 turns (Opus 4.8), incl. a judge-panel workflow (4 angles) + adversarial abort-path check | LIVE-verified: a 15s-cap 6-host batch aborted 5 hosts, returnedToPending=40 (not failed), fetched rows still markComplete (26+23 redirect), and RUNNING:0 after — the leak is gone; DB-evidence confirmed no lost/double leases (the only stale in-progress were 12 Guardian rows from a batch I killed mid-flight via restart — reclaimed by the already-wired recoverStale-on-hydrate at the 20min lease timeout); all 7 run-probes green. 158 pages/55.1 MB crawled, archive 77,801 | SCAFFOLD: resilience-wiring probe extended to assert the abort wiring (registry.stop + returnedToPending) so a refactor can't silently drop it; brainstorm captured as docs/plans/2026-07-autonomous-crawl-adaptation.md. | 2026-07-21 | **Distributed crawling on Oracle — research + unification plan (Opus 4.8, cycle 64, planning/architecture).** Owner asked how to increase pace via the remote Oracle machine — distributed crawls with per-IP + overall rate limiting, a remote page queue, 15s-1min batch delivery, N-pages-per-HTTP-request, and a jsgui3 progress dashboard deployed ON the remote ("unifying some code"). Ran **3 parallel research agents** (remote-fetch architecture; rate-limiting architecture; Oracle deployment + prior work) → the honest finding: **most of what's wanted is ALREADY BUILT but fragmented across 3 never-unified generations** (Gen1 orphan; Gen2 v2 server DEPLOYED on Oracle with remote queue + watermark batch export at ~5s target, but dual-DB + pull + approval-gated; Gen3 stateless /batch worker with N-bodies-in-1-gzip-response + single local DB, but its fetchBatch/queueRequest are BUILT-BUT-DEAD — live crawl sends batch-of-1). Rate limiting has **NO cross-node coordination** (2 nodes → 2× a host's rate → 429/blackout; per-process backoff invisible cross-node) — the ONE safety-critical missing piece. Plan (docs/plans/2026-07-distributed-crawl-unification.md): unify on one remote server + auto-driven on-by-default export→ingest + wire the dead batch paths + a shared **politeness broker** (per-host nextAllowedFetchAt + shared backoff via the DomainThrottleManager.limiterFactory seam) + a **shared jsgui3 dashboard** served from the remote. | ~3 turns (Opus 4.8, mostly parallel research) | Persisted plan (6 phases D1-D6, acceptance + risks); the pace answer = UNIFY + WIRE what exists (v2 already meets 15-60s) + the broker (unlocks safe 2-node throughput) + the remote UI, NOT new machinery; 9/9 probes; crawl landed real headlines ("Alleged Maduro co-conspirator says CIA knew about coup plans", "Slovakia passes constitutional amendment…", "Real Madrid's Mbappe out of Spanish Super Cup derby…") +3 section/utility collapsed by FIX 18; archive guardian 61,983 docs | Planning cycle (no code shipped — the deliverable is the grounded plan, which prevents rebuilding already-built infra + pinpoints the safety-critical gap). | 2026-07-21 | **Phase D2a — remote-node politeness: code shipped + tested; deploy HELD for owner confirmation (Sonnet 5, cycle 67).** The confirmed finding: the deployed remote worker (`run-worker.js`) fetched with ZERO robots/crawl-delay compliance — a strict sequential loop, `redirect:'follow'`, no pacing at all. **Investigated first, built second:** confirmed `DomainLimiter` + `parseCrawlDelay` are both standalone/pure (no dependency on the heavy local CrawlerState/wiring apparatus), so they drop into the lean single-file worker cleanly — reuse, not reinvention. **Also discovered same-host serialization was ALREADY structurally guaranteed** (one PM2 process per domain; `startDomain()` refuses a second start while `running`; the fetch loop is a strict sequential `while`, no `Promise.all`) — so no new concurrency-pool machinery was needed, correcting an assumption in the plan's phase description. Shipped: **`remote-politeness.js`** (RemotePoliteness: real robots.txt Crawl-delay parsing, default 1s safety floor — NEVER zero, adaptive 429 backoff via the local DomainLimiter) + **`polite-fetch.js`** (politeFetch: `redirect:'manual'`, gates EVERY hop including redirects, **blocks and records** any redirect landing off the worker's exclusively-owned domain instead of auto-following it — closing the silent cross-host-fetch leak `redirect:'follow'` created) + **`host-match.js`** (extracted, shared). Wired into `run-worker.js` (politeFetch replaces the old unpaced fetchWithTimeout; a blocked cross-host target is recorded as a discovered link for local re-queue via the existing sync path, never fetched here). **Closed the LOCAL fail-open seam too:** `DomainThrottleManager.acquireToken`'s degraded-limiter fallback was silent — now counts + logs loudly (`degradedLimiterAcquireFailures`), behavior unchanged. **Investigated `/api/tools/fetch` per the plan's explicit ask ("gate every outbound fetch including this") — found it ALREADY BROKEN independent of politeness** (`CrawlWorker` is referenced but never defined/imported anywhere in the repo; every real domain's worker is a plain stub with no `processSingleUrl`) — documented with a code comment rather than building a new feature to "fix" a dead endpoint (scope discipline). | ~2.5 turns (Sonnet 5) | **27/27 new/modified unit tests** (8 RemotePoliteness: real Crawl-delay parsing, never-falls-back-to-zero, caching, actual pacing math verified by hand-calc, 429 delegation, snapshot; | 2026-07-21 | **Phase D3 — sync-loop hardening, all 4 sub-fixes shipped + tested (Sonnet 5, cycle 68).** No explicit deploy go-ahead was present, so D2a's `--apply` stayed held (per plan); moved to D3 using the code-complete D2a as foundation. **(a) Watermark timing:** confirmed the causal chain in code (`since = ... getLastWatermark(ledger) ...` at the sync loop's next-round call site) — `appendBatch` advanced `lastWatermark` immediately, so a crash between append and confirm left the watermark already past an unconfirmed batch, silently skipping it FOREVER on restart. Fixed: watermark now advances only in `markConfirmed`, using the entry's own stored watermark; monotonic (never regresses on out-of-order confirms). **(b) Identity-strict verify:** deleted both fallbacks in `legacy-remoteCrawlSyncVerification.ts` (the "latest response for this URL" fallback + the size-only content fallback) — both could false-positive-verify a batch that was NEVER ingested against an unrelated existing row, letting prune delete the remote's only copy of real data. Also fixed the matching loose fallback inside the ingest file's own copy of the same lookup (consistency). **(c) Idempotent ingest — SCOPE-ADJUSTED with live evidence:** the plan called for UNIQUE indexes on http_responses/content_storage, but a bounded read-only probe against the LIVE 30GB news.db found **25,267 pre-existing duplicate `(url_id, fetched_at, request_started_at)` groups already there** — a `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX` would fail outright without a destructive dedup pass first (filed separately, needs owner approval; NOT attempted this cycle). Shipped the safer alternative instead: a purely-additive `remote_sync_batches(batch_key PK)` table keyed by a content-derived fingerprint (sha256 of sorted remote httpResponse ids), checked before and recorded INSIDE the same transaction as the batch's inserts — a replay is a fast no-op, and a failed transaction correctly does NOT mark the batch ingested (proven: a forced-failure test shows a retry re-attempts for real). **(d) Corrupt-ledger quarantine+halt:** `loadLedger`'s catch-all used to silently return `emptyLedger()` for ANY error including a genuinely unparseable file — now a missing file still safely empties/migrates, but an unparseable EXISTING file is renamed aside (`.corrupt-<ts>`) and throws `LedgerCorruptError` (loud, actionable) instead of silently wiping sync state. | ~2.5 turns (Sonnet 5) | ncdb: tsc clean; rewrote 2 existing tests that had encoded the now-removed fallback behavior as their fixtures (a null-sha "match" and an identity-free lookup) — replaced with realistic fixtures + 4 NEW adversarial tests (a never-ingested batch must fail verify even when an unrelated same-URL response exists locally; a missing content_sha256 must count as missing, not size-match); **verification file: 6/6**, **ingest file: 8/8** (incl. the transaction-atomicity proof); **full ncdb sqlite suite: 133/133 files, 791/791 tests** (zero regressions); copilot: sync-ledger 9/9 new tests (watermark-on-confirm, the exact crash-recovery scenario reproduced, monotonic advance, corrupt-file quarantine, valid round-trip, legacy-format migration); 3 syntax checks; 10/10 probes; crawl landed real headlines ("Live updates: Collision between helicopter and plane kills 67…", "Budget airline Avelo to fly deportation flights for ICE from Arizona") +4 collapsed by FIX 18; archive apnews 8,410 docs (+6) / guardian 61,991 | **D3 complete.** No destructive remote ops run (verify-then-prune still gated; the live-DB dedup for (c)'s original unique-index design is filed as a separate owner-decision task with hard evidence). Next: D2a deploy (if owner confirms) or D4 (shared jsgui3 dashboard core) |

| 2026-07-21 | **Owner-reported metrics-accuracy fix: phantom "Saved docs/s" (Fable 5, cycle 69).** Owner: "the displayed metrics… Saved docs/s appears to be wrong" — verified while running a live crawl. ROOT CAUSE, two layers, both confirmed live before fixing: (1) PRODUCER — jobProgress.js retains last-window delta rates (anti-flicker) but never zeroes them at job end; the final progress event is typically a terminal reconcile where saved jumps while downloaded does not, freezing docsSavedPerSec>0/docsDownloadedPerSec=0 into the completed job snapshot forever (live-observed ghosts: 1.47, 2.46, 0.08 on completed jobs). (2) CONSUMER — renderThroughput summed rate fields across ALL jobs in the list (13 jobs, mostly completed) → the strip displayed Σ of frozen ghosts while the crawler was idle, and finished jobs stale queued counts polluted the Queue tile. FIX at both layers: InProcessCrawlJobRegistry._publicProgress zeroes the 4 rate keys for terminal jobs at the SINGLE serialization seam (covers every terminal path + every consumer; cumulative totals untouched — they are still true; internal record not mutated), + renderThroughput filters to ACTIVE jobs only (client half also protects against an older server build). | ~2 turns (Fable 5) | Reproduce-then-refute LIVE: pre-fix jobs payload showed completed jobs with nonzero docsSavedPerSec; post-fix sampling across a fresh crawl: doneJobsWithNonzeroRate=0 across all samples AND running jobs still show real internally-consistent rates (apnews dl=0.68/s sv=0.34/s with 2-of-4 saved; thehindu dl=0.63 sv=0.47 with 3-of-5) — proving no over-zeroing; after all jobs finished the strip truthfully reads 0.00/0.00/0.00/0.00/0 (screenshot). Tests: 7 new terminal-rate registry tests + 2 new phantom-rate client render tests (10/10 suite) + 22/22 sibling regression; SSR check green (template-trap tripwire); 10/10 probes; crawls landed real headlines ("After tear gas and street fires, a Los Angeles community cleans up…", "Government forces Air Canada and flight attendants back to work…") +3 collapsed by FIX 18; archive guardian 61,992 docs | 4th crawl-status failure mode banked in [[crawl-status-client-template-trap]] (backslash-eating → apiBasePath-scope → contrast → phantom rates). Dead-code discovery filed as a chip: CrawlerMetricsService recorders are never called. D2a deploy still HELD (no go-ahead in the message); D4 next unless redirected |

| 2026-07-22 | **Phase D2a DEPLOYED to live Oracle PM2 — but only after DB-evidence caught a false "deploy complete" and fixed TWO real defects (Fable 5, cycle 70).** Owner gave the explicit go-ahead ("Deploy whatever is needed, whenever you need"). **Defect 1 — the entire D2a politeness was dead on arrival.** The deploy tool's `runCommand('ssh', …, installScript)` passes the multi-line bash install script as a single argv element while running with `shell:true` on Windows (needed so bare `ssh`/`scp`/`tar` resolve via PATHEXT) — cmd.exe mangled the script, so the remote install silently no-op'd and the tool printed "complete" with the buildId unchanged (May-27). Diagnosed by SSHing in to read actual file mtimes + PM2 uptime, then hand-deployed. **The recursive prompt's own acceptance step then caught Defect 2:** the newly-landed worker crash-looped (15 restarts) with `Cannot find module '../../../src/core/crawler/limiter'` — `remote-politeness.js` (cycle 67) required three helpers by a `../../../src/…` path that reaches OUTSIDE the deploy package; the tarball never included `src/core/crawler/*` and the install script even `rm -rf src`. So buildId flipping ≠ code live: only the seeded test crawl proved the politeness path was inert (the "50 fetched @ 200ms" log lines were the OLD pre-D2a worker in the ring buffer). **Both fixed properly:** (1) vendored `{limiter,RobotsCache,utils}.js` byte-identical into `deploy/remote-crawler-v2/lib/vendor/` (recursively staged by the deploy tool) + repointed the requires + a `vendor-sync.test.js` drift guard that fails the moment an original diverges; (2) fixed the deploy tool to deliver the install script over **ssh stdin to `bash -s`** (raw bytes cmd.exe can't touch) via a new `input` option on `runCommand`. Re-ran the REAL deploy tool end-to-end (validating the fix): new buildId `20260721233634`, `--db data/news-simple.db` preserved, 0 restarts. | ~2 turns (Fable 5) | **DB-evidence acceptance (the decisive proof):** seeded bbc.com on the partition, ran the worker, then computed per-fetch deltas from the remote `http_responses`/`urls` join — `request_started_at` median **1.04s** (last-15: 0.83–1.36s, the 1s floor + ~40ms jitter), `fetched_at` median **1.00s** — vs the old pre-D2a worker's **200ms interval / 300 rpm**. Worker now `online` 0-restarts (was errored/15). Tests: remote-politeness 8/8 (mock path repointed to `../vendor/utils`) + vendor-sync 3/3 drift guard; 5 syntax checks; deploy tool `node --check`. Real news this turn: **~140 bbc.com pages** at polite cadence ("Is it better to shower in the morning or at night?", "How the seasons change our sleep", BBC language editions) + local orient crawl headlines (AP "Voters reject stadium tax for Royals and Chiefs", The Hindu, Irish Times, Guardian); archives grew (local news.db 30 GB, remote news-simple.db 2.6 GB). | **D2a live + verified.** The deploy tool is now reliable on Windows (stdin-piped install). Partition reverted to committed default (remoteHosts `[]`). Lesson banked: buildId change is necessary-not-sufficient — a cross-package require can pass every local check and still MODULE_NOT_FOUND on the box; the seeded-crawl acceptance is what caught it. Split 2 composite tests along the module boundary and moved 1 wholesale, updating scripts/deploy-remote.js's stale path reference. **Phase B (the new capability):** a `GzipPool` over `worker_threads` (pool size min(cpus-1,4), ArrayBuffer transfer with a pool-safety isolation copy, respawn-once/dead-after-2nd-crash, queued-job-rejection-on-close to prevent a hang) replacing the inline synchronous `zlib.gzipSync` in the fetch loop — sha256 now computed BEFORE the buffer is transferred/detached (order matters), row-scoped values snapshotted into the async closure to avoid a loop-variable-capture bug. **Self-caught 2 real bugs before they shipped**: a queued-job-forever hang in `close()` (fixed by rejecting undispatched queue items rather than awaiting them), and a genuine `spawnSync` event-loop deadlock while writing the end-to-end integration test (a fixture HTTP server in the same process as the `spawnSync` call can never be serviced while spawnSync blocks the loop — reproduced, root-caused, fixed with async `spawn()`, banked as memory). **Phase C (rewiring):** repointed the deploy tool's staging source to the module while keeping the ON-BOX layout byte-identical by design (so the install script/PM2-entry/rm-list needed zero edits) — caught and fixed a real staging leak (a test-only crash fixture shipping in the tarball) via a `fixtures` directory exclusion. **Phase D (deploy):** real `--apply` deploy succeeded (buildId changed, PM2 0 restarts on first start); DB-evidence proved the compression pool works on the LIVE box (~83-87% real size reduction on genuine bbc.com/theguardian.com/apnews.com content) alongside intact 1s-floor politeness (median ~1.04s deltas). **Phase E (the crawl):** 5 domains seeded+started; Also discovered and documented (not a regression — a pre-existing architectural fact newly load-bearing for verification): `run-worker.js` is a one-shot script, so PM2's `fork_mode` autorestart cycles it on every clean completion — worker restart COUNT is consequently NOT a crash signal once a crawl has run a while, only on the very first post-deploy start. | ~3 turns (Sonnet 5, model-swap, ultracode) | Standalone: `news-crawler-itself` 45/45 tests (pool unit 12, correctness+parity, close-drain incl. the fixed hang, crash/respawn, 2 real end-to-end integration tests via the actual child-process entry point against a real HTTP fixture, + 3 relocated test files). copilot-dl-news side: 11/11 relocated/split tests green, deploy tool's own 23/23 green after the packageShape edit. | 2026-07-22 | **OWNER-directed UI fix: "make sure crawl activity is displayed correctly" — the home Recent Crawl Activity table was reading a stale WRONG source; repointed it to the live job registry + hit and dodged the template trap (Opus 4.8, cycle 78, ultracode).** The owner's directive preempted the planned focus (cycle-62 precedent: a user-facing regression on an owned pillar comes first). LOOKED at the live UI first: the "Recent Crawl Activity" table showed a task-id TIMESTAMP (`2026-05-13T03-24-04-278Z`) as a crawl "target", `example.com/`, EVERY row labelled "Active", and durations of 0s — while "Active Crawls: 0" sat directly above it (a self-contradiction). Root cause (traced, not guessed): the table was server-rendered in `subApps/registry.js` from `db.taskEvents.listRecentCrawlTaskRuns` — the STALE `task_events` table (months-old campaign runs), whose `targetHost` fell back to the task-id when no start-config was captured (→ the timestamp) and whose status defaulted to "Active" when no terminal event existed. Meanwhile the ACTUAL recent crawls (today's `basicArticleCrawl` worker jobs) live in the in-process job registry that `/api/v1/crawl/jobs` and the "Active Crawls" stat already use. **Fix:** removed the stale server-side task_events render, marked the `<tbody data-home-activity-body>`, and hydrated it CLIENT-SIDE from `/api/v1/crawl/jobs` inside the existing 5s `bindCrawlNavBadgePoll` refresh — real target (host+path), real status (running→Active/completed→Complete/failed→Failed, honest terminal states), real live-updating duration, HTML-escaped. **Hit the [[crawl-status-client-template-trap]] again in a NEW file:** `UnifiedShell.js`'s inline script is emitted from a backtick template literal, so my `replace(/^https?:\/\/(www\.)?/, '')` + `replace(/\/$/, '')` emitted as broken regexes — the second (`replace(//$/,`) a `//` line comment that would have broken the whole client script. Caught it by VERIFYING THE EMITTED TEXT (extracted the `<script>`, saw the mangled regex), rewrote with backslash-free string ops (`indexOf('://')`/`slice`), and re-verified the emitted script parses. | ~1 turn (Opus 4.8, ultracode) | Live browser verification (screenshot): "Active Crawls: 1" now MATCHES the table showing `theguardian.com/politics` **Active** (green, live 54s) + `france24.com/fr` **Complete** — no timestamp-targets, no example.com, no everything-Active; 0 console errors; the running job later flipped to terminal correctly. EMITTED client script extracted + `vm.Script`-parsed clean (template trap dodged). New regression test `UnifiedShell.activity.test.js` (4: emitted-script-parses, live-source-hydration, backslash-free-strip, honest-status) — 4/4. crawl-status-ssr tripwire + all 10 probes green. Standing crawl: run-multi theguardian+france24; archive +79 → 74,098 analysed headlines. Made live via electron restart (httpOk). | **The owner's "displayed correctly" was a real, concrete bug — the activity table and the Active-Crawls stat read DIFFERENT sources and disagreed; now both read the live registry, so the display is internally consistent and truthful.** The template trap recurred in a second file — the "verify the EMITTED text, not just parse success" discipline (memory) caught it again; memory extended to name UnifiedShell.js as a second known template-literal client script + its guard test. Next: D4 slice 2b (task #54), document-intelligence extraction #2, or the crawler-engine discovery-time junk filter (owner-gated data question). |

| 2026-07-22 | **document-intelligence OFFLINE-adoption accuracy gate — measured NO-GO; the module is miscalibrated for news content (Opus 4.8, cycle 81, ultracode).** Cycle 80 blocked the drop-in and said an ADDITIVE-OFFLINE adoption needs a labelled-fixture accuracy pass first (the module mislabels real pages). This cycle RAN that gate, the honest way: used copilot's OWN stored verdict as ground truth — `content_analysis.classification='article'` is 78,889 rows (copilot only runs content-analysis on pages it stored as articles → a strong "this IS an article" label) — pulled 300 confirmed articles + their decompressed HTML, and measured what the module says. **Result: the module calls a confirmed article "article" only 38.0% of the time (114/300) and produces a 39.3% HARD false-positive rate (118/300 real news articles → login_page/product_page/organization_page/error_page)** — e.g. "Trump says China's Xi to visit U.S." → product_page, "Newsom orders California… clearing encampments" → login_page, thehindu Chhattisgarh news → product_page. GATE (≥80% article-recall AND ≤5% hard-FP) → **FAIL**. The module over-fires its distinctive labels on real articles: it is not usable by copilot on ANY path as-is (drop-in blocked cycle 80; offline blocked cycle 81). Committed the gate as a repeatable `doc-intel-diff.js --accuracy` mode so it can be re-run after any upstream recalibration. This is the third measured block of the doc-intelligence module — and the loop-honest conclusion: the two cheap intelligence extractions (url, document) are both fully measured-and-blocked; the module ecosystem's remaining moves are large (D4 remote-serving, places extraction) or await owner direction. | ~1 turn (Opus 4.8, ultracode) | ground-truth accuracy measurement on 300 copilot-confirmed articles (38% recall, 39% hard-FP, GATE FAIL), reproduced by the committed `--accuracy` mode; ground truth chosen honestly (copilot's stored article classification, not circular — the module never sees it); bounded readonly content_storage read + shared decompress util; node --check clean; 10/10 probes; ncdb-debt ratchet green. Standing crawl: run-multi 3 hosts; archive +19 → 74,121 analysed headlines. | **A rigorous accuracy gate returned a decisive, actionable NO-GO — the doc-intelligence module needs news-content recalibration before copilot can adopt it, and now there is a committed one-command gate (`--accuracy`) to re-verify that.** Measuring against the coordinator's OWN confirmed labels (content_analysis) is a clean, non-circular ground truth that a future impure-module scout should reuse. The intelligence-extraction track is now exhausted of cheap wins; next moves per the mission are D4 remote-serving (latent), places-intelligence #3 (highest-risk), feeding the recalibration finding to the module owner, or awaiting owner direction rather than manufacturing bloat. |

| 2026-07-23 | **OWNER-directed live-DB surgery: the gated http_responses dedup EXECUTED losslessly on the live ~28GB news.db via copy→dedup→verify→swap (Opus 4.8, cycle 82, ultracode).** The owner asked what the "25,267-group http_responses dedup" gated item was, then "Is there a guaranteed safe way to fix it?", then "Please do that" — authorizing the destructive op. Did it the safe way: **never mutated the live file** until a verified copy proved lossless. Measured first (the losslessness proof, not assumed): the dup key is `(url_id, fetched_at, request_started_at)`; **0 dup groups held divergent content** (all `content_sha256` equal within a group), so deleting non-keeper rows drops only byte-identical copies. **A full reverse-FK scan caught a landmine a naive delete would have silently detonated:** `article_place_relations.article_id → http_responses` is **ON DELETE CASCADE** — a third child beyond the obvious content_storage→content_analysis (both NO ACTION); the tool re-points such rows to the keeper before deleting (0 rows exposed here, guard in place). Keeper per group = richest-chain-first (analyzed > has-place-relations > has-content > lowest id). The whole mutation ran in **one transaction that rolls back unless the sha-based invariants hold** — `COUNT(DISTINCT content_sha256)` (186,255) and `COUNT(DISTINCT analyzed sha)` (70,907) both UNCHANGED — enforced, not trusted (the content_id-based count is the WRONG invariant: collapsing dup content_ids is the point). Post-VACUUM `foreign_key_check`=0 violations + `integrity_check`=ok. Swap kept the original as `news.db.predup-bak`. Result: **325,614 → 290,144 http_responses (−35,470 redundant)**, −10,537 content_storage, −4,829 content_analysis, 25,351 dup groups → **0**, file 30.24 → 28.89 GB, **0 unique/analyzed content lost**. Live-verified reads (dashboard-model/jobs) AND writes (a real-news crawl added +37 rows, dup_groups STAYS 0, fresh 2026-07-23 headlines flowed: AP Trump–Iran live, Al Jazeera "Hamas's new leader calls for permanent Gaza ceasefire", BBC). Investigating a "12,438-name place" found it was the synthetic sentinel `place_id=999999` ("Earth"/"World" catch-all) with 12,436 DUPLICATE rows. **Owner opened the live-DB mutation gate; executed the cleanup to standard: mapped the full reverse-FK graph, built a dry-run-default tool (`tools/db/cleanup-place-names.js`, in-txn verify-before-commit + rollback), copy→verify→swap (place_names 810,278→797,842, sentinel 12,438→2, null_normalized→0, fk_check 0, quick_check ok, original kept as .pre-placenames-bak).** THEN — checking the cleanup STUCK — found the sentinel regrew 2→4 on restart, traced it to the ROOT CAUSE: ncdb `seedBootstrapData` runs on every DB connection and inserted planet names with `INSERT OR IGNORE` but OMITTED `normalized` → NULL defeats BOTH the UNIQUE(place_id,normalized,lang,name_kind) index AND the OR IGNORE (NULL≠NULL), re-inserting "Earth"/"World" every startup. Fixed it (set `normalized=normalizeName(name)`, rebuilt ncdb dist), proved idempotent (seed 3×→2 rows; restart no longer grows the sentinel), swept the residual, and made the cleanup tool re-runnable. | ~8 turns (Opus 4.8, ultracode; two 28GB copies + slow whole-DB fk_check/quick_check dominate) | owner-caught overstatement corrected across ledger/SELF_MODEL/memory; canonical_name_id display fix + fallback (7/7 place-articles tests); copy-verify-swap place_names cleanup (all 7 invariants + fk_check 0 + quick_check ok, verified on the copy before swap); root-cause fix proven idempotent (seed 3×→2 rows, restart sentinel stays 2, quick_check ok live); cleanup-place-names 10 unit tests; 46/46 intelligence+db tests; app HTTP 200; 3 out-of-scope chips filed (1 completed+dismissed). | **The owner's challenge caught a real overstatement — the correction (verify the baseline is REAL before claiming you beat it) is now banked, and chasing the "why" of a weird data point (12,438 names) led to a genuine recurring-bug fix (a NULL in a UNIQUE-index column silently defeats both the index AND INSERT OR IGNORE — same NULL-key family as the http_responses dedup). | 2026-07-25 | **CRAWL-PERF Step 1: the sitemap workaround WORKS but does NOT fix the wedge — a measured NOT MET that redirects the whole fix queue (Opus 5).** Prior research fingered the sitemap enqueue fan-out (~5,000 URLs × uncached-prepare SELECTs + sync INSERTs, no yields) as the cause of the server child wedging and the job registry emptying. Prime remaining suspect: **DB contention from 10 forked workers** each opening the 27 GiB news.db (74 MiB WAL) with synchronous better-sqlite3 startup queries, blocking the server past the 3 s probe — the wedge mode already documented at `main.js:241-257`. Filed a cheap **concurrency bisect** (same launch at 1/3/5) as the next action, to locate the fix before optimising the wrong path. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium) | override proven non-no-op via startup-stage telemetry + enqueue count 4867→6; failure proven via API (`jobs: 0`) + DB (+12 rows, last fetch 440 s ago) — not log-only (capture hit a 256 KB cap, stated honestly); watchdog strikes counted; per-host table shows ~1 page/host. | **A well-evidenced NOT MET that is worth more than a false pass: it disproves a root cause I had already written into a plan doc, and stops Steps 2/3 (per-URL enqueue cost) being built before the real bottleneck is located. The reusable discipline: a workaround that provably TAKES EFFECT but does not fix the symptom is strong evidence the diagnosis is incomplete — check the symptom, not just the mechanism you changed.** Next: concurrency bisect, then re-order the queue. | -->

| 2026-07-25 | **CRAWL-PERF Step 1b: concurrency bisect returned NON-MONOTONIC results and I caught my own experiment as invalid rather than reporting the numbers (Opus 5).** Ran 1/3/5 sites (distinct sets, sitemaps off, 150 s each): 1 site → jobs 0, +4 pages; **3 sites → 3 jobs ALIVE, +59 pages, last fetch 0 s**; 5 sites → jobs 0, +36 pages. Non-monotonic, so it does not support "more concurrency = wedge". **But the experiment had two disqualifying flaws, both self-caught:** (1) the inter-trial server-child kill silently FAILED — proven by "server ready after **0 s**" on all three trials where a real kill+respawn takes ~9 s warm (likely a path-separator mismatch in the PowerShell `CommandLine -like` filter) — so the trials shared one server and were never isolated; (2) my verdict logic treated `jobs: 0` as "wedged", but it conflates wedged/completed/failed, and **the jobs API responded in every trial**, which a genuinely wedged server could not do. So no wedge was actually confirmed at measurement time. Solid takeaway: a **3-site crawl is demonstrably healthy** (3 alive, +59 pages, 0 s freshness) — the largest configuration proven to work. Also found a new obstacle: today's jobs write **no per-job log files** (newest in `data/logs/jobs/` are Jul 23, all `status=completed`) even though the launch response still advertises `logPath` — the main per-job diagnostic channel is missing. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium) | 3 trials × 150 s observation with DB row deltas + jobs API; invalidity proven by the 0 s restart timing and by the API responding while reporting 0 jobs; job-log absence proven by mtimes. | **Catching that my own measurement was confounded is worth more than the numbers it produced — reporting a non-monotonic "finding" from unisolated trials would have sent the next cycle chasing a phantom. Two reusable checks banked: (a) `jobs: 0` is NOT evidence of a wedge if the API is still answering — a wedged server cannot respond; distinguish wedged/completed/failed explicitly; (b) verify a teardown actually happened (PID changed / restart took plausible time) before trusting trial isolation.** Next: re-run distinguishing terminal job status + server PID change, one trial per server boot. | -->

| 2026-07-25 | **CRAWL-PERF Step 1c: it is a CRASH, not a wedge — and the enabler is that jobs run IN-PROCESS because my own launch method omitted worker mode (Opus 5 + 1 subagent).** Polled the jobs API every 15 s for 300 s while tracking my own job IDs and the port-3170 listener PID via `netstat` (not the command-line match that failed in 1b). Timeline is unambiguous: `PID 3032 → NONE → 53472` — the server child **disappears entirely** and the supervisor respawns it; afterwards the API answers in **2–3 ms**, so nothing is wedged. Death fell between t=15 and t=30, far too fast for the watchdog's 3-strike/~30 s sequence ⇒ **the watchdog was reacting to the death, not causing it; the cycle-1/1b "wedge" framing was wrong.** Second finding: **jobs were mostly not killed — 3 of 5 reached `completed` within 15 s**; only the 2 still `running` were lost. So the tiny page counts (+4, +36) in earlier trials were jobs FINISHING, not being killed. Root enabler found by a parallel subagent (run concurrently with the live trial — independent code-reading question, ~5 min saved): `InProcessCrawlJobRegistry.js:55` gates worker mode on `UI_CRAWL_WORKER === '1'`; `server.js:1348-1354` never passes `workerMode`; and **`UI_CRAWL_WORKER=1` is set only by `dev-bridge.js:231,260`, never by `main.js:67-76`**. Launching Electron directly (as I did all session) therefore runs crawls INSIDE the server child, so any crawl fault kills it — confirmed live by the total absence of `crawl-operation-worker.js` processes. Same cause explains the missing job logs: the per-job WriteStream exists only in `_startOperationInWorker` (:544-552). **My prior hypothesis — the `.on('error')` WriteStream bug at :486 — was WRONG; that bug is fixed (`_guardStreamErrors` :240-254 runs at :550 before any write).** | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium; 1 subagent 71k tokens) | 300 s live poll with per-job status + PID timeline + API latency; PID change proves child replacement; absence of worker processes proves in-process mode; subagent cited file:line for the worker-mode gate and the log-stream location, and explicitly ruled out cwd, flush-on-completion, and the swallowed-stream-error hypotheses. | **Three framings died this cycle and the diagnosis is finally load-bearing: not a wedge (crash), not killed jobs (they completed), and not the WriteStream bug (fixed). The decisive instrument was tracking the LISTENER PID — `jobs: 0` and log evidence were both ambiguous, but a changed PID is unforgeable proof the child was replaced. Also banked: my own launch command was part of the bug surface — reproducing an issue with a hand-rolled launch that skips the project's normal startup path (dev-bridge) can manufacture a failure mode that does not exist in normal operation.** Next: relaunch via dev-bridge (worker mode ON) and re-test — a crawl fault should then kill only a worker. Separately: jobs completing in <15 s with sitemaps off will not reach 200 pages/site even once the crash is fixed. | -->

| 2026-07-25 | **CRAWL-PERF Step 1d: FIXED — restoring worker mode makes the crawl work; the whole failure was my own launch method (Opus 5).** Relaunched Electron with `UI_CRAWL_WORKER=1` exported (inherited by the server child via `main.js:69-73`), then ran 5×200 polling every 20 s for 300 s. Crawls then run in forked workers, so a crawl fault kills only a worker — server, job registry and UI all survive. **Problem (B) dissolved too:** jobs no longer finish in <15 s, so "instant completion" was a symptom of in-process mode, not of sitemaps being off. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium) | 300 s poll of three independent signals (listener PID via netstat, worker-process count, job-log file count) + post-run DB check (605 pages/286.8 MB/16 s freshness, per-host breakdown) + jobs API showing 5 tracked jobs. | **Four cycles chased a "sitemap fan-out wedge" that did not exist as described — the real defect was that MY launch command skipped the project's normal startup path, running crawls inside the server so any fault killed it. The generalizable lesson, now proven expensively: when reproducing a failure, use the project's OWN startup path first; a hand-rolled launch can manufacture a failure mode that never occurs in normal operation, and every downstream diagnosis then describes an artefact. Also: three independent signals (PID / worker procs / log files) converged, which is what finally made the verdict unambiguous where single signals (jobs=0, logs) had each been misleading.** Still open: this run had sitemaps OFF, so whether the enqueue fan-out (Steps 2/3) destabilises a WORKER is untested — next test is worker mode + sitemaps ON at 10×200, i.e. the original goal. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF Step 4: measured NO-GO on ANALYZE — and the measurement retired Steps 2 and 3 as well; there is no DB performance problem (Opus 5).** Instead of paying a 27 GiB copy up front, asked the cheaper prior question — *is the planner actually choosing badly?* Read-only `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` on the live DB: **every table in both per-URL hot queries already uses a COVERING INDEX with zero statistics** (`sqlite_autoindex_urls_1`, `idx_http_responses_url_status`, `idx_content_storage_http_response`), running at **0.013 ms** and **0.032 ms per call**. So ANALYZE has no upside while carrying real downside (stats can flip a good plan bad) plus a gated live write — **recommend not running it.** Then quantified Step 2 directly: cached statement **0.0113 ms/call** vs `prepare()`-per-call **0.0272 ms/call** — 2.4× slower but only **0.016 ms** absolute, i.e. **136 ms vs 57 ms across a full 5,000-URL sitemap ingest, saving ~79 ms.** Negligible. **This also corrects the cycle-1 research**, which characterised the per-URL enqueue work as "minutes of a fully wedged event loop"; measured, the DB portion is ~0.03 ms/URL ⇒ **~150 ms for 5,000 URLs — an over-estimate of ~3 orders of magnitude.** Step 3's justification is likewise gone (1e showed no worker stalls; the fan-out is cheap). Two lessons banked: (a) a ratio can be true and irrelevant — 2.4× slower sounded actionable until the absolute (0.016 ms) was computed; ALWAYS convert a ratio to absolute cost against the real workload before scheduling work; (b) prior research that was never numerically checked over-estimated a cost by ~1000× and drove four cycles of plans — treat unmeasured cost claims in research output as hypotheses, not findings.** Remaining DB work (Step 5 pragmas) has correspondingly limited upside and mmap still risks the DB-swap workflow; treat as optional and evidence-driven. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 8: NOT MET — and it invalidated every throughput number I reported in cycles 5-7 (Opus 5).** Owner set a standing 1.2 MiB/s DL cap; I flagged up front that this sits BELOW the 1,652 KB/s baseline so the "does host selection raise peak throughput?" question becomes unmeasurable, and reframed to "does it hold the cap steadily?". What SURVIVES: everything proven by PID / worker-count / job-status / page-count evidence — the crash diagnosis, the worker-mode fix, stability. Only rate numbers fall. Second error: the trial was confounded — 35 jobs (27 running) because cycle 7's crawl was never stopped. Corrected indicative reading: ~480-520 KB/s, ~40% of the 1.2 MiB/s cap, so the cap is not the binding constraint. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium) | side-by-side raw-vs-`datetime()`-wrapped counts at the same instant across 2/5/10-min windows; format-mix census (202,176 vs 93,278); job census showing the 27-running confound; internal-inconsistency check (240% of a hard cap) as the trigger. | **The most valuable outcome available this cycle was destroying my own numbers rather than defending them — and the trigger was a consistency check, not new data: a rate that exceeds a HARD CAP is impossible, so the instrument is wrong, full stop. Bank the reflex: sanity-check every measurement against a known physical bound before interpreting it. Worse, this repo ALREADY fixed this exact bug (ledger #36, crawl-rate.js timezone-string-compare) and my own memory documents it (`pm2-log-and-timestamp-verification-traps` — "always wrap datetime() on BOTH sides, never raw string >=") — I reproduced it from scratch anyway. | 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 9 / Step 9: MET — a trustworthy throughput number at last (≈375 KB/s, 31% of cap), obtained by triangulating three instruments that fail differently (Opus 5).** After cycle 8 invalidated every rate figure, the fix was not merely to wrap `datetime()` but to stop trusting any single instrument. Measured the same run with **A** own SQL (`datetime()` both sides), **B** `/api/v1/crawl-rate-timeseries` (ncdb-owned, per-minute buckets, already ledger-#36-correct), and **C** the registry's per-job `bytes` counters — **C never reads a timestamp at all, so it is structurally immune to this entire bug class.** Pre-flight *asserted* the clean start (`jobs=0`, `workers=0`, else abort) rather than assuming it, closing cycle 8's second defect. **Result: A and B diverge by 1-2% and C tracks both** — 5-min 340 vs 348 KB/s, 10-min 380 vs 375 KB/s (910/946 fetches); nothing exceeded the cap; PID 47556 unchanged for the full 600 s; 13-15 workers throughout. Per-minute buckets `151,301,488,434,638,360,316,359,342,365` show a physically sane ramp-peak-settle — **the shape alone would have exposed cycle 8's fake monotonic climb on sight, which the aggregate-only view hid.** **Headline finding: at 15 concurrent hosts the crawl uses under a third of the 1.2 MiB/s allowance, so the cap is NOT the binding constraint and raising it would change nothing — per-host politeness is, and the only real lever is more hosts in parallel** (consistent with #45 and `crawl-gap-is-politeness-not-deadtime`). Honest limit stated: this is a trustworthy number *for ranked hosts*, which is what Step 9 asked; it does **not** show ranking beats not-ranking, since the unranked arm exists only in invalidated form. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium) | 3-instrument agreement (A-vs-B 1-2%, C tracking); pre-flight clean-start assertion with abort; cap verified via API response (`rateBytesPerSec: 1258291.2`); every rate cap-checked (>105% prints "INSTRUMENT BROKEN, not a finding"); per-minute bucket shape reviewed; PID unchanged 600 s; live headlines confirm real news landed. | **The durable lesson from the cycle-8 failure was not "wrap datetime()" — that fixes one bug. It was that an instrument cannot validate itself, so I built the trial around instruments with DIFFERENT failure modes, and their agreement is what makes 375 KB/s believable where 1,652 KB/s never was. Two reusable moves banked: (a) prefer an instrument that structurally cannot have the bug — instrument C ignores timestamps entirely, so no amount of format chaos can fool it; (b) keep the raw SHAPE, not just the aggregate — the per-minute buckets make an impossible trend self-evident, whereas a single averaged number can be wrong quietly. Also: the harness now ASSERTS its preconditions and aborts, converting last cycle's confound from a thing I must remember into a thing the tool refuses to let happen.** Next: A/B ranked-vs-arbitrary 15 hosts to finally answer the selection question, and reconcile `frontier-hosts.js` BACKLOG ranking against observed SPEED (it omits bbc/dw, the fastest hosts seen). | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 10 / Step 10: the planned 3-arm live trial was REFUTED BEFORE RUNNING, and the question it existed to answer was settled from existing data instead — backlog-ranking does NOT predict speed (Spearman ρ = −0.093, n=18) (Opus 5).** Spent one subagent on adversarial design review *before* the ~35 min experiment, then verified its load-bearing claims myself rather than taking them at face value. Three independent defects, all confirmed: (1) **Arm B cannot exist** — `frontier-hosts.js --limit 30` returns **20** hosts, the entire proven-crawlable population (gate `proven>=1000 AND frontier>=5000`, `frontier-hosts.js:64`), so "ranks 16-30" is 5 hosts and would confound ranking with host count — the very variable Arm C existed to isolate; (2) **the null result was guaranteed** — measured per-host politeness-bound supply (avg bytes/fetch ÷ mean gap, last 60k responses, PK-bounded) puts Arm-A hosts at **3,233 KB/s = 2.6× the cap and 8.6× the 375 KB/s observed**, so the manipulated variable acts on a resource nowhere near binding and every arm would land on the same downstream ceiling; (3) **the arms would overlap** — `crawl-batch.js` is fire-and-forget (`process.exit()` at :412), jobs end only at their 200-page budget (cycle 9 had 12/15 still running past the window), so Arm C inherits A+B residuals and is biased upward by pure accumulation, spuriously confirming its own hypothesis. **The retrospective answer, ~0.2 s and no crawl: ρ = −0.093.** Guardian is rank 1 by backlog and 15th of 18 by speed (21 s mean gap); CNN is rank 9 by backlog and 1st by speed by 16×. Not a defect in the ranker — it optimizes *article backlog* and was explicitly built (2026-07-21) to deprioritize hub-heavy hosts for headline quality; it was never a speed ranking. Also found: **CNN alone is 77% of Arm-A byte supply** (3,533 KB avg page vs BBC 271 KB, >20× spread), so any KB/s arm score would mostly measure "was CNN in this arm". Caveats stated: supply extrapolates observed rate as sustainable, and excluding CNN the margin is 1.9× not 8.6× (728 KB/s) — "not host-supply-bound" survives, its size doesn't. **Correction issued to cycle 9:** instruments A and B are NOT independent (the timeseries endpoint queries `http_responses`, same source as my SQL); their agreement proved the `datetime()` fix and precision, not attribution — only instrument C (registry byte counters over worker IPC) is genuinely independent, and it is what makes 375 KB/s trustworthy. Number stands, stated reasoning was overstated. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium) + 1 subagent | independently re-derived supply (3,233 KB/s vs reviewer's 3,290), concentration (CNN 77%) and ρ = −0.093 with my own PK-bounded window-function query; confirmed pool=20 by running the ranker; confirmed fire-and-forget at crawl-batch.js:412 and the `allowMultiJobs` default-false gate at server.js:1352; one reviewer claim (preferCache file paths) did NOT match the tree and is recorded as directionally-confirmed-only. | **The highest-value thing this cycle was NOT running the experiment. An adversarial review before an expensive trial cost one subagent and saved a 35-minute run that was mathematically incapable of producing an interpretable answer — and the same analysis that killed it also ANSWERED the underlying question at ~zero cost, which is the "cheap prior question" discipline paying off twice in one move. Two lessons banked: (a) before manipulating a variable, check that the resource it acts on is actually BINDING — supply was 2.6× the cap, so no arm could have differed and the null would have been an artifact I'd have reported as a finding; (b) I validated the reviewer rather than deferring, which caught that one of its citations didn't match the tree — a subagent's verdict is evidence, not authority. Also: my own cycle-9 independence claim was wrong and a reviewer caught it; two instruments over the same table measure precision, not attribution.** ⇒ Queue reordered: the bottleneck is downstream of both host supply and the cap (event-loop ceiling ~0.43 MB/s, where 375 KB/s sits), so raising process headroom now PRECEDES any further host-selection work. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 11 / Step 11: bottleneck NAMED and QUANTIFIED — 68% of inter-fetch time is idle that is unexplained by round-trip time plus configured crawl-delay; the "electron event-loop ceiling" framing is RETIRED as the wrong instrument (Opus 5).** Steady-state baseline established honestly: contiguous 7-min block, 856 fetches, **481 KB/s = 39% of the 1.2 MiB/s cap**, reconciled to 0.0% drift. Gap decomposition: actual download **1-3%**, TTFB 10-30%, configured crawl-delay ~0% (13/15 hosts have none), **unexplained idle 68%**. **The natural experiment that makes it solid:** `robots_cache` has **0 rows**, and the only two hosts with a stored delay are the only two whose gaps are fully explained — Straits Times (10s configured, **0.0s** unexplained) and RTE (5s, **0.2s**). Where a delay is configured the throttle honours it exactly; where none is, the crawler idles anyway. **Ruled out by measurement, not assumption:** bandwidth (39% of cap), host supply (cycle 10: 2.6x cap), CPU (32 cores, nowhere near saturation), politeness (robots_cache empty), rate limiting (telegraph/irishtimes/euronews/independent all carry `safe_rpm=300` yet show 1.8s/14.9s/13.7s/19.2s gaps — same nominal limit, 10x different behaviour), and serial latency (download is 1-3% of the gap; the connection is idle, not busy). **Leading hypothesis, explicitly NOT proven: per-host queue starvation** — gap is inversely related to fetch count (telegraph 167 fetches @ 1.8s vs independent 20 @ 19.2s), the signature of some hosts having ready work and others waiting. Jobs were homepage-seeded, so a thin homepage starves its host. **Fix PROPOSED, nothing shipped** (step scope): keep every host's queue non-empty via continuous hydration. **Prediction stated in advance: 481 -> ~1,200 KB/s, where the cap finally binds (~2.5x); if it lands and throughput does NOT move, the hypothesis is wrong and profiling becomes justified.** Incidental, not chased: robots_cache empty though robots is parsed in-memory per job (`CrawlerServiceWiring.js:353`) — re-fetched every job, never persisted. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (short sequential measurement chain, each query decided the next) | v3 harness asserts per-host fetch counts reconcile to the block total and ABORTS on >5% drift (came out 0.0%); every rate sanity-checked against the cap; the two-host configured-delay natural experiment cross-validates the whole attribution | **I made TWO measurement bugs this cycle and caught both before reporting — the harness discipline is now load-bearing, not decorative.** (1) v1 averaged per-host gaps across a 30-min drain tail at 1-2 fetches/min, manufacturing fake slack in 19 of 22 hosts — caught by reading the minute-by-minute SHAPE, which is exactly why keeping the raw shape is a rule. (2) v2 selected 29 SCATTERED steady minutes then queried the whole 131-min span between the first and last, dividing 7,551 fetches by 29 minutes of time and producing **103% of a hard cap** — caught by the above-cap sanity check firing exactly as designed. Fix was structural, not vigilance: v3 uses a contiguous block AND a reconciliation assertion that aborts. **Lesson: "steady state" is not a filter over minutes, it is a CONTIGUOUS interval — a scattered selection silently divides a wide numerator by a narrow denominator.** Also: my crude CV classifier split 7-vs-8 on an arbitrary threshold and mislabelled france24 (CV 2.08) as "regular"; I discarded the label and read the raw distribution instead, which is what surfaced the median-far-below-mean burst/pause signature. A classifier you wrote five minutes ago is not evidence. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 12 / Step 12: queue-starvation hypothesis FALSIFIED, and cycle 11's registered prediction WITHDRAWN before a line of code was written (Opus 5).** The falsifier was clean and cost one API call: **the jobs completed their full 200-page budget** (nytimes, irishtimes, apnews, independent, thehindu all hit exactly 200). A starved job ENDS EARLY with fewer pages; these never ran out of URLs. So cycle 11's proposed "continuous per-host queue hydration" would have solved a problem that does not exist — the pre-registered 481 → ~1,200 KB/s prediction is withdrawn, not chased. **Actual cause, attributed to two intentional code defaults:** (1) `concurrency: {default: 1}` at `NewsCrawler.js:215` — each host job holds ONE request in flight, fetches are serial, and `crawl-batch --concurrency 15` does NOT override it (it sets parallel START requests, per cycle 10); (2) `rpm: 30` "Default conservative RPM for new domains to prevent 429 errors" in `DomainThrottleManager.getDomainState` — a **2 s floor** between requests. **Confirmation that #2 binds:** the two fastest hosts sit exactly on the floor — Telegraph 1.8 s (~33 rpm, RTT 0.58 s) and ABC 2.2 s (~27 rpm, RTT 0.47 s); both could go 2-3× faster on RTT alone. Critically, **for these hosts the throttle binds BEFORE concurrency does** (2 s floor > 0.58 s RTT), so raising concurrency alone would change nothing — the cycle-10 "check which resource is actually binding" discipline applied before proposing, not after. **Secondary defect found: stored rate limits are not reaching the limiter, and host-key form is inconsistent** — `domain_rate_limits` holds rows under both bare and `www.` forms (`telegraph.co.uk` preset 25 vs `www.irishtimes.com` learned 300; `abc.net.au` has no row), and hosts with `learned_rpm=300` were observed at 13.7 s and 14.9 s, ~70× slower than their own stored limit. **Residual stated honestly:** the slow population (euronews 13.7 s at 0.2 s RTT, independent 19.2 s at 0.11 s RTT) is slower than concurrency=1, the 30 rpm floor AND their stored limits can explain; two distinct populations exist and the defaults explain only the fast one. Per-page post-fetch work is the untested candidate, needing a per-page timing instrument rather than more DB archaeology — and those hosts contributed only 20-29 fetches, so their gap is noisy and must not be over-read. **NOTHING SHIPPED: the fix is gated on an owner decision because it is not purely technical** — raising concurrency or the 30 rpm default makes us hit third-party publishers harder, and that default exists precisely to prevent 429s. Host-key normalisation is a plain bug and safe to fix either way. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (short sequential chain, each query chose the next) | falsifier is job-level page counts (200/200), independent of every DB timestamp instrument used in cycles 9-11; the binding-resource check was run BEFORE proposing a fix | **A hypothesis I had proposed a fix for one cycle earlier died to a one-call check I could have run before proposing it. The cheap falsifier — "if starved, the job would have ENDED EARLY" — was available the whole time and is structurally independent of every instrument I had been building.** Lesson: when forming a hypothesis, immediately ask what OBSERVABLE would be different if it were false, and prefer a falsifier that lives in a different data source than the one that generated the hypothesis (job page-counts, not fetch timestamps). Second lesson, carried forward and applied correctly this time: I checked WHICH of the two candidate limiters actually binds (throttle at 2 s vs concurrency at 0.58 s RTT) before recommending a change, which prevented proposing a concurrency increase that would have moved nothing for the fastest hosts. Third: the fix is a POLITENESS-POLICY change with outward-facing consequences for real publishers, so it stops at a recommendation and goes to the owner — speed work does not get to quietly decide how hard we hit third parties. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 13 / Step 13: PARTIAL. Concurrency 4 doubled one host with ZERO 429s; the arm total is confounded by my own design error. Owner-approved fix (a) NOT implemented because inspection showed it is a speed REGRESSION, not a fix (Opus 5).** **Correction to my own cycle-12 attribution:** `rpm: 30` does NOT pace un-429'd hosts — `limiter.js:50-53` early-returns with ZERO delay when `!isLimited && politenessFloorMs <= 0`, and `rateInterval` is additionally gated on `isLimited` (`limiter.js:59`), set only by a 429. The default is INERT. Telegraph's 1.75 s gap is fetch (~0.67 s) + ~1.1 s of post-fetch PROCESSING serialised behind `concurrency: 1`, not a throttle. **Correction to the approved scope:** nothing in the crawl path reads `domain_rate_limits` — grep for `learned_rpm`/`safe_rpm` across `src/` returns nothing and ncdb's `getDomainRateLimit` (`coverage.ts:322`) has no callers; `limiter.js` holds state in a bare in-memory Map, fresh each crawl. **So the stored presets (Telegraph 25 rpm, Independent 30, Al Jazeera 40) are ALREADY ignored and we fetch faster than they specify — wiring them in is a politeness TIGHTENING that would slow crawling.** I did not implement it: it cannot deliver what it was approved for, and shipping it silently would have slowed the crawl while I reported it as a speed fix. Sent back for a fresh owner decision under its true description. **Correction to cycle 12's falsifier:** "completed 200/200" rules out TERMINAL starvation only — a job can stall repeatedly on link discovery and still reach budget; intermittent starvation remains live, and cycle 13 then observed terminal starvation directly. **A/B (2 hosts, only concurrency varied, `preferCache=false` pinned both arms, ZERO code changed — ramped purely via `--override concurrency=N`):** abc.net.au 205 fetches @ 2.05 s gap → **417 fetches @ 1.01 s gap = 2.03x, gap exactly halved, 0 × 429**, matching the pre-registered "overlappable idle" prediction; its rtt rose 0.76 → 1.75 s (server-side queuing = the host IS being pushed harder, still no 429s). **The arm TOTAL (306 → 300 KB/s) is INVALID and must not be read as "no effect":** (1) order-induced depletion — arm A downloaded 240 Telegraph pages, arm B found them already fetched (`UNDOWNLOADED = NOT EXISTS`, `legacy-crawlFrontier.ts:61`) and Telegraph exhausted after 29 pages; cycle 10's review warned of this carry-over and I failed to control it; (2) both arm-B jobs completed by t=300 s so fetches froze at 446 while the denominator grew — the printed rate falls 501→300 arithmetically, not physically. Normalised to ACTIVE time: arm B 1.49 fetches/s vs arm A 1.06/s (~1.4x) even with Telegraph crippled. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (focused change + measurement) | pre-registered the prediction BEFORE running and both branches were falsifiable; 429s counted by PK range (immune to the mixed-timestamp column); pre-flight asserted jobs=0/workers=0 and would have aborted; zero production code changed — the ramp used an existing `--override` | **The summary line said 300 vs 306 KB/s — "no effect" — and that reading would have been wrong in both directions. Reading the per-host rows and the SHAPE instead showed one host doubled and the other had been depleted by the preceding arm. If I had trusted the aggregate I would have reported a real 2x gain as a null result and abandoned the correct lever.** Lessons: (a) an aggregate over a window whose jobs FINISH EARLY silently divides fixed work by growing time — assert jobs are still `running` at the window's end or discard the window; (b) sequential arms against a shared, depleting resource are confounded by construction — counterbalance the order or give each arm untouched hosts, which cycle 10's review told me and I still got wrong; (c) **an approved fix must be re-verified against the code before implementing — the approval was given under a description that inspection proved false, and implementing it as approved would have made things worse.** Approval is not a substitute for checking. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 14 / Step 14A: stored rate-limit floor IMPLEMENTED with 14 passing tests and a real-DB-verified provider — but LIVE ENFORCEMENT IS UNCONFIRMED and is recorded as such. 14B not done (Opus 5).** Implements owner decision #2 ("wire them in, be more polite, accept slower"): `DomainThrottleManager.normalizeHostKey()` (lowercase + strip ONE leading `www.`), `storedRowToFloorMs()` (explicit crawl_delay_seconds > safe_rpm > learned_rpm), `_effectiveFloor()` combining robots and stored floors by **max()** — chosen because the owner asked to be MORE polite, so combining can only slow us and still satisfies "robots is an absolute floor" in the direction that matters — plus a `storedRateLimitProvider` injected at `CrawlerServiceWiring.js:295`, consulted once per host, cached, with **any failure leaving pacing exactly as before**. 14 new tests pass (25 in file; 11 pre-existing still green): normalisation, single-www stripping, row→floor, precedence BOTH directions, no-row, no-provider, throwing provider, once-per-host. Provider SQL verified against the live DB: `www.telegraph.co.uk` → `telegraph.co.uk` → 2400 ms (a row exact-string lookup missed), `www.abc.net.au` → genuinely no row. **The bug live verification caught that unit tests could not: I ASSUMED `crawler.dbAdapter.db` was a better-sqlite3 handle; it is a NewsDatabaseFacade whose own `.db` is the raw handle.** The provider silently returned null and no floor was ever applied — exposed by a pre-registered falsifiable threshold (a 2.4 s floor caps Telegraph at 150 fetches/360 s; observed **174**). Fixed by RESOLVING the handle (`a.db.db` → `a.db` → `a`, first with `.prepare`) rather than guessing a third time. **Re-verification was INCONCLUSIVE and is not being recorded as success:** Telegraph — the only tested host where the floor binds — is depleted (24 fetches then `completed`), far below the 150-fetch threshold, so the run cannot distinguish "floor applied" from "no URLs left"; the ABC control behaved normally at 1.86 s. Next-cycle confirmation specified: assert the provider resolves a `.prepare`-capable handle from a real `createCrawlerDb(...)` (deterministic, no crawl), then end-to-end on a NON-depleted preset host (`bbc.com` 2000 ms / `aljazeera.com` 1500 ms). **Step 14B (counterbalanced concurrency re-run) NOT done** — the 14A verification loop consumed the cycle; the confounded cycle-13 total stands uncorrected. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (14B's design was already fully specified in the prompt, leaving little for a review to catch) | 14 unit tests + provider verified against real DB rows + a pre-registered numeric falsifier (≤150 fetches) that actually FIRED and caught the integration bug; safe failure mode means the tree is not broken while unconfirmed | **Unit tests passing and a standalone-verified SQL query still added up to a feature that did nothing, because the seam between them — the adapter's shape — was assumed rather than checked. The only thing that caught it was a pre-registered NUMERIC threshold on live behaviour ("≤150 fetches or the floor isn't on"), which is why a falsifiable number beats "looks right".** Lessons: (a) when wiring into an unfamiliar object, RESOLVE the capability you need (probe for `.prepare`) instead of asserting a path — I guessed `.db` twice; (b) a verification host must be capable of producing the observation — Telegraph was depleted, so the re-run was uninformative and I had to say so rather than let a quiet result read as success; (c) **a safe failure mode (provider error ⇒ unchanged pacing) is what makes it acceptable to leave this half-verified in the tree overnight**, and is worth designing in before the first live test, not after. | -->
