| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 15 / Step 15: stored rate-limit floor CONFIRMED WORKING deterministically — real adapter → real DomainLimiter → an actual 2400 ms wait — with 40 green tests, no crawl and no live-DB write. Also found: the change will rarely BIND on today's hosts (Opus 5).** **The fix that made confirmation possible was structural, not more testing:** the provider was extracted from `CrawlerServiceWiring` into `src/core/crawler/storedRateLimitProvider.js`. Cycle 14's inline version was a silent no-op **past 14 green tests** because every one injected a FAKE provider and none touched a real adapter — a test of a COPY of the logic guards nothing. `resolveSqliteHandle()` now PROBES for a callable `prepare` across `adapter.db.db → adapter.db → adapter` rather than asserting a path. Chain now proven by test end-to-end: real `createCrawlerDb()` adapter → handle resolved → row found → floor computed → **real DomainLimiter** → measured 2400 ms sleep between consecutive fetches (mocked clock, so deterministic). Regression guards: `resolveSqliteHandle` returns null for a NewsDatabase-shaped wrapper with no `prepare` (the exact cycle-14 failure); **a no-row host does NOT wait at all**, locking in cycle 13's finding that `limiter.js:50-53` early-returns with zero delay and is why `rpm: 30` was inert; robots wins when stricter; null/non-queryable/throwing adapter all → no floor. **Discovery worth more than the feature: a live end-to-end test is STRUCTURALLY IMPOSSIBLE on current hosts.** For the floor to be observable a host must naturally fetch FASTER than its preset, and no non-depleted host does — BBC 5.3 s natural vs 2.0 s floor, Al Jazeera 4.2 s vs 1.5 s, Independent 19.2 s vs 2.0 s; Telegraph (1.75 s vs 2.4 s) was the only one it would ever slow, and it is depleted. **⇒ owner decision #2 is correctly implemented but will rarely bind; the forecast aggregate throughput drop is ~zero on today's hosts, not the meaningful slowdown predicted in cycle 14.** That correction was reported rather than left to look like a delivered slowdown. **14B (trustworthy concurrency number) still NOT done**; specified a cheaper sound design that removes the order confound entirely — ONE run, four hosts, split arms (two at concurrency=1 and two at concurrency=4 launched simultaneously) so neither arm can deplete the other's hosts and both see identical network conditions. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (short verification chain, each result chose the next) | 40 tests green (12 provider/seam + 28 throttle); the decisive one asserts a real 2400 ms WAIT via the real limiter, not merely a recorded field; zero live-DB writes and zero crawls needed | **Cycle 14's lesson was "verify live"; the better lesson turned out to be "make the seam testable". The inline provider could not be tested against a real adapter without extracting it — so extraction, not another crawl, is what actually closed the gap.** Second: I nearly ran a live confirmation that could not have confirmed anything, and only caught it by checking each candidate host's NATURAL pace against its floor first — a verification host must be capable of producing the observation, and here NONE was. Checking that beforehand converted a wasted 20-minute run into a real finding (the change rarely binds). Third: when a feature turns out not to matter much in practice, say so plainly — the owner accepted a throughput cost that will not actually materialise, and letting that stand would be a quiet misrepresentation of what their decision bought. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 16 / Step 16: split-arm trial executed cleanly, pre-registered threshold PASSED on the aggregate (1.54x vs a 1.3x bar) — and I called it NOT MET, because the per-host rows refute it (Opus 5).** The design fixed cycle 13's flaw: simultaneous launch on DISJOINT hosts removed the order/depletion confound, all 4 jobs were still `running` at the window's end (informativeness assertion passed), 429s were 0 in both arms, and hosts were pre-screened so the cycle-15 stored floor could not bind even at 4x (`natural_gap/4 >> floor`) — a confound the prompt did not flag and that would have suppressed the very effect being measured. **Result: A(conc1) 0.886 fetches/s → B(conc4) 1.367 = 1.54x.** **Why I rejected my own passing result:** (1) `www.smh.com.au` effectively FAILED — 17 fetches, active only 45 s of a 480 s window — so arm B is `npr` alone, yet smh's 45-second sliver carries equal weight in the arm sum; **excluding it the ratio is 1.12x, BELOW the falsifier**, so the headline rests on a dead host; (2) with smh gone the matched pairing broke, making it a cross-host comparison (npr vs abc+thehindu) rather than a controlled one; (3) the 24 h "natural gap" baselines used to pair hosts are unreliable — thehindu ran 1.65 s against a 5.7 s baseline **at concurrency 1**, because a 24 h average blends multi-host contention with idle periods. Consistent-with-treatment but not proof: rtt rose in arm B (npr 2.19 s, smh 2.93 s) exactly as cycle-13's abc did (0.76 → 1.75 s), the server-side-queuing signature. **ROOT CAUSE identified across two failed attempts: per-host supply/failure variance swamps the treatment effect in ANY between-host design at this sample size** — cycle 13 died to depletion, cycle 16 to a host dying. Specified the redo: **within-host ABA crossover** on one proven-reliable high-volume host (npr/thehindu both sustained 290-455 fetches), `conc=1 → conc=4 → conc=1` in three ~4-min blocks, so host identity is held constant and the A-B-A shape separates treatment from time drift; **inclusion rules declared in advance** (a block counts only at ≥50 fetches AND ≥240 s active, else report underpowered) — which would have auto-excluded smh instead of letting it drive the headline. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (design was specified in the prompt; I added the floor-interaction screen myself before running) | all 4 jobs verified `running` at window end · 429 counted per arm by PK range · active-time normalisation per host · pre-registered numeric falsifier · floor-cannot-bind host pre-screen | **The aggregate said REPLICATES and I had a pre-registered threshold saying I could bank it. Reading the per-entity rows — my own standing rule — showed the number hinged on a host that ran for 45 seconds, and that dropping it flips the verdict to 1.12x. A pre-registered threshold is necessary but NOT sufficient: it protects against post-hoc goalpost-moving, not against a broken sample.** Two lessons banked: (a) **pre-register INCLUSION rules alongside the threshold** — "a unit counts only with ≥N observations and ≥T active time" — because a threshold applied to a degenerate sample is still a wrong answer; (b) after two between-host failures from different causes (depletion, host death), the design class itself is wrong at this sample size — **switch to a WITHIN-unit crossover rather than iterating on host matching**. Recognising a failing design CLASS, instead of patching the latest symptom, is the actual progress this cycle. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 17 / Step 17: ABA crossover NOT MET — but it measured the NOISE FLOOR (1.6× same-condition variance) which retroactively explains all three failed concurrency cycles: n=1 per condition was never enough (Opus 5).** Blocks: A1 conc=1 → 135 fetches/234 s/0.577 per s; **B conc=4 → 2 fetches/15 s, job died**; A3 conc=1 → 218 fetches/237 s/0.920 per s. 429s ZERO in all blocks. **MY OWN DESIGN ERROR, stated plainly: the pre-registered inclusion rule was arithmetically UNSATISFIABLE** — I required ≥240 s active inside a **240 s** window, but "active" is first-fetch→last-fetch and is necessarily shorter than the window, so **no block could ever pass**; A1 and A3 missed by 6 s and 3 s. A gate that cannot be satisfied is not a gate. Fix recorded: express inclusion as a FRACTION of the window (≥60 %), never as an absolute equal to it. Block B collapsed to 2 fetches and terminated — the third host to die under test — but **NOT to depletion**, since A3 ran immediately afterwards on the same host and was the strongest block of the three. **THE REAL RESULT: A1 and A3 are the SAME host at the SAME concurrency 12 minutes apart and differ by 1.6× (0.577 vs 0.920 per s) — a same-condition noise floor LARGER than the 1.3× effect being hunted.** That single number explains every prior failure better than the per-cycle diagnoses did: cycle 13 blamed depletion, cycle 16 blamed a dead host and broken sample, cycle 17 blamed an impossible gate and a dead block — **all symptoms; `n=1 per condition` was the disease.** No arrangement of arms can resolve a ~1.5× effect against ~1.6× noise at one block per condition. Specified the powered redo: replicated alternation `A B A B A B` (3 per condition) on npr, 3-minute blocks, comparing condition MEANS with spread reported, inclusion as a fraction, a condition counting only with ≥2 surviving blocks — resolving ~≥1.5× and no finer, stated up front. Escalation named if that fails: stop measuring concurrency against live publishers and build a controlled offline fixture harness where network and publisher variance are removed entirely. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (design was specified; the failure was in my own gate arithmetic, which a reviewer plausibly WOULD have caught — noted for next time) | per-block PK-watermarked stats · active-time normalisation · pre-flight jobs=0/workers=0 asserted and passed for all 3 blocks · stillRunning captured per block · 429 counted per block | **Three cycles of increasingly careful designs each failed, and each time I diagnosed the proximate cause (depletion → dead host → dead block) and iterated on the arrangement. The right move was to measure the NOISE first: one pair of same-condition blocks would have shown 1.6× variance and proved on day one that every planned design was underpowered. Measure the noise floor BEFORE designing the comparison — effect size means nothing without it.** Second lesson: **a pre-registered gate must be checked for satisfiability** — I wrote a rule that no possible run could pass, and it silently invalidated the protection I had built. Third: I have now spent four cycles failing to measure one number against live third-party servers; the discipline being learned is to recognise when the ENVIRONMENT, not the design, is the obstacle, and to move to a controlled fixture rather than iterate a fifth time. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 18 / Step 18: offline fixture harness BUILT + VALIDATED, and it settled in ONE run the number five live cycles could not: concurrency 4 = 3.23×, instrument noise 5.6% vs 60% live (Opus 5).** Shipped `tools/perf/fixture-server.js` (deterministic local site; controllable latency and page size; **counts its own requests and tracks maxInFlight**) + `tools/perf/concurrency-bench.js` (interleaved replicates, reports the noise floor BEFORE any comparison) + `tools/perf/fixture-crawl-runner.js`. Results: conc=1 → 5.53 req/s (spread 1.6%, maxInFlight 1); conc=4 → **17.85 req/s** (spread 5.6%, maxInFlight 4). **Effect 3.23×, exceeding instrument noise by 40×**; replicates 5.50/5.50/5.59 and 17.89/17.33/18.32, so drop-one passes trivially (worst-case pairing still 3.10×). **maxInFlight 1-vs-4 PROVES the setting took effect — a check no live cycle could make**, and the reason earlier nulls were uninterpretable. **A real bug the fixture caught in its first run, which every live cycle would have hidden: `src/crawl.js` hard-codes its config path (`crawl.js:34`) and merges argv as `[...configArgv, ...directArgv]` with the config's `startUrl` FIRST (`configArgs.js:237`), so the first positional wins and a start URL passed on the command line is SILENTLY IGNORED — the first benchmark run crawled https://www.theguardian.com instead of the fixture.** The fixture's own request counter (0 requests) exposed it immediately; fixed by constructing NewsCrawler directly in a runner, because a benchmark must not depend on ambient config. **Bonus quantification for the open slow-host item: at conc=1, 5.53/s = 181 ms/page against 120 ms injected latency ⇒ per-page post-fetch processing ≈ 61 ms; at conc=4, 56 ms/page = 81% of the ideal 4× (22.1/s), so processing partly SERIALISES — that is why the gain is 3.23× not 4×.** **Scope stated honestly: 3.23× is an UPPER BOUND, not a live prediction** — the fixture has no robots crawl-delay, no stored floor and uniform page weight, and cycle 15 showed most preset hosts already run slower than their floors, which caps the benefit entirely for them. Proposed (GATED, owner approval required): make concurrency 3-4 the default — 3.23× fixture, ~2× live on abc.net.au, and **zero 429s across every cycle of this workstream**; robots floor and 429 backoff untouched, since concurrency overlaps waiting rather than shortening any configured delay. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (build + validate chain; each failure chose the next step) | fixture counts requests independently of the news DB (immune to the timestamp/attribution bug class) · maxInFlight confirms the treatment applied · noise floor measured BEFORE the comparison · interleaved replicates · drop-one robustness · smoke test before the real run | **Five cycles failed to measure one number against live publishers; the fixture answered it in a single run, and the deciding factor was an instrument that could VERIFY THE TREATMENT WAS APPLIED (maxInFlight) rather than only measure the outcome. Every earlier null was ambiguous between "no effect" and "setting never took effect" — and the very first fixture run proved that ambiguity was real, because the crawler had been silently ignoring my start URL and hitting theguardian.com.** Lessons: (a) **when you cannot distinguish "no effect" from "not applied", build the instrument that observes application, not a better comparison**; (b) a benchmark must not inherit ambient config — hard-coded config paths turn a controlled experiment into an uncontrolled one without any error; (c) the owner's call to stop iterating live and build a fixture was right, and I should have proposed it after the second failure rather than the fourth. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 19 / Step 19: live 429-safety validation CLEAN — zero 429/403/503 across 3,419 responses on 7 hosts at concurrency 3; and closed an incoherence in my own proposal by measuring the fixture at the SAME concurrency that was safety-validated (2.88×) (Opus 5).** Design insight that made the cycle work: **match the METRIC to the noise.** Live rate is undecidable at the measured 60% same-condition variance, but **429s are a COUNT, not a rate**, so they are entirely immune to that variance — a live run therefore CAN decide "is this polite enough?" even though it cannot decide "how much faster?". Ran 7 hosts deliberately mixing preset-floor (npr, irishtimes, cbc, aljazeera) and no-floor (abc, thehindu, cna) for 16 min at concurrency 3. **The abort rule (any host reaching 3× 429/403/503 ⇒ kill workers immediately) was ENFORCED IN CODE rather than merely reported, because the run put extra load on real publishers** — it never tripped. Result: 3,419 responses, **0 × 429, 0 × 403, 0 × 503**. **Self-caught incoherence: my cycle-18 proposal quoted 3.23× (concurrency 4) alongside safety evidence gathered at concurrency 3** — two different settings presented as one recommendation. Fixed by measuring conc 3 on the fixture: **2.88× (15.81 vs 5.48 req/s, noise 4.7%, maxInFlight=3 confirming it applied)**. That also yields the argument for the conservative default: **3 → 4 buys only +12% while raising per-host load 33%.** Incidental, flagged not chased: `www.cbc.ca` returned 404 for 89 of 459 requests (19%), far above other hosts — a link-construction/normalisation smell, not a politeness signal. Re-proposed (owner-gated): default concurrency 3. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (design risk was concentrated in the abort rule and host list, both of which I made conservative; spent the budget on enforcing the abort in code instead of reviewing it) | abort rule enforced in code with worker kill · status mix counted per host by PK range (a COUNT, immune to the timestamp bug class) · pre-flight jobs=0/workers=0 · hosts mixed across floor/no-floor · fixture re-run at the validated concurrency so speed and safety describe the same setting | **The cycle's real value was noticing that my own recommendation quoted a speed number for one setting and a safety number for another. It reads fine in a summary and is meaningless as a recommendation — the kind of error that survives precisely because both halves are individually true.** Lesson: **when combining evidence from two runs into one proposal, check they describe the SAME configuration** before presenting them together. Second: matching the metric to the environment's noise (counts survive where rates do not) turned an "unmeasurable live" situation into a decisive answer — the question was answerable all along, just not the question I had been asking. Third: for an outward-facing run, an abort rule that only reports is not a safety mechanism; enforce it in code. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 19b: owner approved and SHIPPED the concurrency default 1 → 3 — the first throughput change of this entire workstream to land in production code (Opus 5).** `NewsCrawler.js` default changed with the full evidence recorded at the change site (2.88× fixture with `maxInFlight=3` proving it applied; zero 429/403/503 across 3,419 live responses on 7 mixed hosts; 3 rather than 4 because 4 buys only +12% for 33% more per-host load), plus an explicit note that this does NOT loosen politeness — robots crawl-delay stays an absolute floor and 429 backoff is untouched, because concurrency overlaps WAITING rather than shortening any configured delay. Added a guard test asserting the default is 3, since a silent revert would give back a measured ~3× of throughput. 41 tests green. **Checked rather than assumed: `gazetteer/__tests__/concurrency.behavior.test.js` fails at module load inside jsdom (`Tests: 0 total`) — I stashed my change, re-ran, and confirmed it fails IDENTICALLY without it**, so it is pre-existing and unrelated; recorded as a separate issue rather than quietly attributed to my change or ignored. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium) | guard test on the default value · 41 tests green · pre-existing failure isolated by stash-and-rerun rather than assumed · evidence written at the change site so the next reader sees why | **Twelve cycles of measurement produced exactly one line of shipped production change — and that ratio is correct, not wasteful. The line is defensible because every claim behind it was verified at the setting actually shipped: the speed number, the safety number and the default now all describe concurrency 3. Earlier versions of this same recommendation quoted a fixture number for concurrency 4 beside live safety data for 3, which read fine and meant nothing.** Also: when a test failed right after my edit, the cheap correct move was to stash and re-run rather than reason about whether a default value could plausibly break jsdom — 30 seconds of evidence beats a confident argument. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 20 / Step 20: per-page post-fetch cost DECOMPOSED offline — parse dominates (~26 ms of ~35 ms non-latency) and scales hard with page weight; DB share measured but REJECTED as too thin to claim (Opus 5).** Built `tools/perf/postfetch-cost.js`: 3 interleaved arms × 3 reps against the fixture, latency held at 5 ms so processing dominates, `enableDb` on/off isolating persistence, 15 KB vs 120 KB isolating parse scaling. Results: db-on-normal 39.8 ms/page (spread 14.3%), db-off-normal 30.6 ms (12.3%), db-on-large 62.8 ms (21.9%). **RESOLVABLE: 8× page bytes adds +23.1 ms/page — 2.7× the noise band and a 58% increase in per-page cost.** **REJECTED MY OWN TOOL'S VERDICT: the DB share came out 9.2 ms (23%) against a ±8.7 ms noise band, and the script printed "RESOLVABLE" at a ratio of 1.06× — too thin to accept, so it is recorded as suggestive only.** Also noted that this instrument is much noisier (12-22%) than the concurrency bench (0.9-5.6%) because 40-page arms make per-page timing far more sensitive. **⇒ ~26 ms of the ~35 ms non-latency per-page cost is parse + link-extraction, single-threaded CPU work — which EXPLAINS the 81%-of-ideal concurrency ceiling from cycle 18: extra parallel fetches cannot overlap CPU already saturating the thread.** Connects two earlier findings: cycle 10 measured CNN ~3.5 MB/page vs BBC 271 KB (>20× spread), so **heavy pages cost twice — bandwidth AND parse time.** Next lever named with a precedent in-codebase (move parsing to worker threads, as cycle 73 Phase B did for compression), plus a cheaper thing to try first (skip full DOM parse for pages already classified non-article). Noted that ledger #46 already collapsed a triple `cheerio.load`, so the easy parse win is spent. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline, cheap, single measurement chain) | 3 interleaved arms × 3 reps · per-arm spread reported · every difference compared against the noise band before being claimed · fixture request counter independent of the news DB · async spawn (fixture server is in-process) | **The tool I wrote printed "RESOLVABLE" for the DB share and I overruled it. A 1.06× margin over the noise is not a finding, and the fact that my own script's threshold was too permissive is exactly the kind of thing that slips through when the author and the reviewer are the same. Writing the noise band next to every difference is what made overruling it possible — the discipline is not "compute the noise", it is "print it beside the claim so the claim cannot be read without it".** Second: this cycle CLOSED a loop rather than opening one — the 81% concurrency ceiling measured in cycle 18 now has a mechanism (single-threaded parse), which converts a curiosity into a named next lever with a precedent in the codebase. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 21 / Step 21: confirmed the DB share (6.3 ms/page) and RETRACTED cycle 20's parse-scaling headline - 23.1 ms was a 5.6x overestimate; the true value is 4.1 ms (Opus 5).** Re-ran `postfetch-cost.js` at **5 reps x 100 pages** (was 3 x 40) and replaced the crude worst-arm-spread heuristic with a proper **2x combined standard-error** test printed beside every claim. Results: db-on-normal 31.4 ms (spread 4.6%), db-off-normal 25.0 ms (8.2%), db-on-large 35.4 ms (3.3%). **PERSISTENCE 6.3 ms/page (20%), +/-0.9 ms, margin 6.98x - RESOLVABLE**; cycle 20 rejected it at 9.2 ms as within noise, which was the CORRECT call on that evidence - the effect is real but smaller than the noisy estimate. **PARSE GROWTH 4.1 ms per 8x page size, +/-0.7 ms, margin 6.06x - resolvable but tiny, versus the 23.1 ms reported last cycle. RETRACTED, along with the heavy-pages-cost-twice claim.** Corrected decomposition of ~26 ms processing/page: **~20 ms fixed per-page work (dominant), 6.3 ms persistence, 4.1 ms size-dependent parse.** Absolute costs also fell (39.8 -> 31.4 ms same arm) because 40-page runs amortise crawler startup poorly, inflating every cycle-20 figure. **=> THE LEVER CHANGED: cycle 20 pointed at page-weight-driven parse cost and that target is now largely gone; the real target is ~20 ms of FIXED per-page work paid regardless of size.** Optimising for heavy pages would have been near-pointless - which is exactly why the cheap confirmatory step was run BEFORE the build step. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline, single measurement chain) | 5 reps x 100 pages, per-arm spread reported, 2x combined-SE test replacing the heuristic that misled cycle 20, every difference printed with its band and margin, fixture counter independent of the news DB | **Cycle 20 printed its noise band honestly, applied it, and STILL produced a number wrong by 5.6x. More replicates did not merely narrow the band - they MOVED the point estimate. That is the sharper lesson: a difference can clear a significance check and still be badly wrong when the instrument is underpowered, because with few samples the point estimate itself is unstable, not just its interval.** Practical rule banked: for a difference you intend to ACT on, first check the estimate is STABLE by increasing n and confirming it does not move - a stability check, not just a significance check. Second: running the cheap confirmatory step before the build step paid for itself immediately - it destroyed the premise of the planned page-weight parse optimisation before any code was written. Third: rejecting the DB share last cycle was right on the evidence available then; being later proven wrong by better data is not a reasoning error, and the ledger should distinguish those. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 22 / Step 22: per-page CPU PROFILED via a two-point diff - total 27.4 ms/page independently confirms cycle 21's ~26 ms, and the top contributor turns out to be IDLE (37%), which WEAKENS my own cycle-21 framing (Opus 5).** Built `tools/perf/profile-crawl.js`: profiles at 100 and 400 pages and diffs per-function self time so module-loading startup is identical in both runs and cancels out. **27.4 ms/page attributed, versus 26 ms measured by timing in cycle 21 - two methods with different failure modes converging, the strongest validation in this workstream.** Findings: **`(idle)` @ native = 10.16 ms/page (37.1%) - NOT CPU**, the process simply is not executing JS; filesystem stat calls (`existsSync` 0.85 + `internalModuleStat` 0.48 + `stat` 0.38) = **~1.7 ms/page (6%)**, which a crawl should not be paying per page; better-sqlite3 wrappers (`exec` 1.41 + `prepare` 0.46) ~1.9 ms, higher than my own bucket reported; **jsdom** DOM construction present in the hot path (`createWindow`, `installInterfaces`, `SymbolTree`, `_stateComment`) where I had assumed cheerio; cheerio/parse5 only 0.08 ms, consistent with cycle 21 retracting the parse-scaling claim. **=> REFRAMING: over a third of per-page time is WAITING, not computing — so cycle 21's "~20 ms of single-threaded CPU that cannot be overlapped" is too strong, and a substantial part CAN be overlapped, which is why concurrency 3 delivers 2.88x.** **Stated limitation rather than glossed: 42.5% fell into "other app code" — the keyword bucketer is too coarse on the largest slice and it misattributed DB work (the `wrappers.js` frames are better-sqlite3 but did not match the DB pattern), so the top-18 function list is the reliable deliverable and the bucket table must not be trusted below its top rows.** Costed proposal: remove the per-page filesystem stats, pre-registered at -1.5 to -1.7 ms/page (6%), to be verified at >=5 reps x >=100 pages with a stability check. Next refinement named: group the unattributed 42.5% by SOURCE FILE within `src/` instead of by keyword. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline, sequential) | two-point profile diff cancels startup, cross-validated against an independent timing method (27.4 vs 26 ms/page), per-function self time from raw V8 samples, async spawn (fixture server in-process) | **The profile contradicted the framing I had written one cycle earlier, and the contradiction is the most useful output: 37% idle means the bottleneck is not purely CPU, so "move parsing to worker threads" - which I had named as the next lever - would address a smaller share than I implied. Two independent methods agreeing on the TOTAL (27.4 vs 26 ms) while disagreeing with my INTERPRETATION of it is a reminder that a validated measurement does not validate the story told about it.** Second: I reported that 42.5% of the time is unattributed rather than presenting the buckets as a complete picture - a coarse classifier that names 57% is a partial result, and calling it a full one would have quietly misdirected the next cycle. Third: cheerio at 0.08 ms/page while jsdom is in the hot path suggests the DOM library actually used in this path is not the one I assumed - worth confirming before any parse optimisation. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 23 / Step 23: per-page attribution COMPLETED - cycle 22's opaque "42.5% other app code" turned out to be a CLASSIFIER FAILURE, not unknown code; and the profile proved my cheerio assumption wrong before it could waste an optimisation (Opus 5).** Extended `profile-crawl.js` with by-source-file and by-area rollups: **53.0% attributed to named source files (13.18 ms/page), 47% native/runtime — nothing left unexplained.** Breakdown: `(idle)` 8.93 ms (35.9%); **DB CPU ~2.7 ms** (`wrappers.js` 1.79 + `legacy-SQLiteNewsDatabase.js` 0.69 + `legacy-ArticleOperations.js` 0.18); **jsdom DOM ~2.3 ms** (Window/utils/nwsapi/interfaces/Element/SymbolTree/HTMLCollection/CSSStyleDeclaration); **filesystem stat ~1.3 ms** (`existsSync` 1.02 + `stat` 0.31); GC 0.60; **Readability.js 0.30**; cheerio/parse5 0.13. **FINDING 1 - the DB reconciles across two independent methods AND explains part of the idle: cycle 21 measured persistence at 6.3 ms WALL, this profile finds only ~2.7 ms of CPU, and the ~3.6 ms gap is waiting on synchronous SQLite disk I/O, which a CPU profile records as idle.** So a substantial share of the 36% idle is the DB rather than the network, making the DB a bigger real target than its CPU share alone suggests. **FINDING 2 - the cheerio assumption was WRONG: the article path runs jsdom (~2.3 ms) plus Mozilla Readability (0.30 ms), while cheerio is 0.13 ms and essentially absent.** Cycle 20's proposed parse optimisation and ledger #46's cheerio work target a library that barely features in this path. Instrument note recorded: total 24.9 ms/page this run vs 27.4 previously (~9% run-to-run), so shares carry ~+/-10% - adequate for RANKING levers, not for claiming small differences. Ranked levers now evidence-based: DB ~6.3 ms wall (largest, attacks both CPU and I/O halves) > jsdom ~2.3 ms (likely structural, Readability needs a real DOM) > fs-stat ~1.3 ms (smallest and cleanest). | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline, one measurement chain) | by-file rollup replaces keyword guessing; attribution total reported (53% named + 47% native = complete); cross-method reconciliation of the DB figure (6.3 ms wall vs 2.7 ms CPU) explained rather than left as a discrepancy; instrument's own run-to-run variation stated | **The by-file view cost one small edit and dissolved a number I had reported as a genuine unknown. "42.5% unattributed" was never unattributed - my classifier just could not name it, and I published that limitation as if it were a property of the system rather than of my tool. Lesson: when a large share lands in an "other" bucket, suspect the CLASSIFIER before concluding anything about the subject.** Second, and more valuable: the profile killed the cheerio assumption that had survived three cycles of planning. Cycle 20 proposed a parse optimisation, cycle 22 named worker-threads-for-parsing as the next lever, and both were aimed at a library responsible for 0.5% of per-page time. **Confirming which code path is actually hot cost minutes and prevented an optimisation that would have measured zero.** Third: a discrepancy between two methods (6.3 ms wall vs 2.7 ms CPU) was worth RECONCILING rather than picking a winner - the gap itself was the finding, since it located ~3.6 ms of synchronous disk I/O hiding inside the idle bucket. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 24 / Step 24: rewrote ncdb `upsertUrl` (correctness VERIFIED 10/10), but the performance claim is UNVERIFIED and I am reporting it as such - the machine slowed ~2.5x mid-cycle and the ncdb test suite could not be run to completion (Opus 5).** **Defect found, worse than expected:** `upsertUrl` called `db.exec()` on a 4-statement STRING-INTERPOLATED blob plus a separately `prepare`d 5th statement, on every call - `exec` cannot use prepared statements, so SQLite re-parsed and re-compiled all of it per URL and the same row was looked up by string key five times (profiled cycle 23: `exec` 1.28-1.79 ms/page + `prepare` 0.51 ms/page). **Change:** one cached `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT(url) DO UPDATE` with bound parameters (UNIQUE constraint confirmed via `sqlite_autoindex_urls_1`), which also retires hand-rolled `'` escaping that was embedding crawled third-party strings directly into SQL text. **CORRECTNESS VERIFIED - 10/10 direct semantics tests** covering the traps that would silently corrupt data: `created_at` unchanged on conflict, `last_seen_at` refreshed, `canonical_url`/`analysis` preserved when the incoming value is null, `host` preserved when the URL will not parse (matching the old try/catch skip), single-quote URLs stored verbatim. **PERFORMANCE UNVERIFIED - before/after INVALID: the `db-off-normal` control arm, which my change cannot affect because persistence is OFF in it, rose 25.0 -> 43.6 ms/page (+74%), and total per-page went 24.9 -> 63.9 ms within the same cycle. The machine slowed ~2.5x between measurements, dwarfing the ~2 ms effect.** Reporting a delta against cycle 21's absolutes would have been reporting machine drift as a result. Frame check also inconclusive, but it explained what survives: `exec` persists because **`upsertDomain` has the IDENTICAL anti-pattern** (untouched), `prepare` persists because ~62 other call sites prepare ad hoc - both code-verifiable, not speculation. **GAP FLAGGED: the ncdb vitest suite crashed (Tinypool) then timed out at 10 min, so the shared-module regression check is INCOMPLETE** - the change rests on direct semantics tests, not the repo's own suite; re-run when the machine is healthy. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline, sequential) | 10/10 semantics tests on a temp DB; UNIQUE constraint confirmed before using ON CONFLICT; control arm used to detect machine drift; working tree verified clean after a timeout interrupted a stash cycle (no leftover stash, TS and dist in sync) | **The control arm saved the report. `db-off-normal` cannot be affected by a persistence change, so its +74% was unambiguous proof that the machine - not the code - had changed. Without an untouched arm in the same run I would have compared to a day-old baseline and published a 64% "regression" that did not exist. Keep a control that the treatment provably cannot touch, and read it FIRST.** Second: a timeout killed a command between `git stash push` and `git stash pop`, leaving the repo mid-surgery; the recovery step was to verify tree state explicitly (stash list empty, diff present, dist in sync) rather than assume. **Any command that stashes and restores must be assumed to die in between.** Third: I shipped a change to a shared module whose own test suite I could not run - defensible here because correctness was directly verified and the change also removes SQL string interpolation of third-party input, but the gap is recorded rather than glossed. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 25 / Step 25: the ncdb "test suite crash" was WATCH MODE, not a crash - suite runs in 145 s and is now fully GREEN (918 passed / 0 failed); perf verdict declared VOID on evidence rather than measured badly (Opus 5).** **(1) Diagnosis:** `package.json` has `test: "vitest"` with no `run` flag, so `npm test` starts a watcher that never exits - cycle 24's "Tinypool crash then 10-minute timeout" was the watcher idling, and the shared-module regression check I had flagged as INCOMPLETE is now closed. **(2) Result: 918 passed / 0 failed / 2 skipped across 156 files.** The one failure present was NOT from cycle 24's `upsertUrl` change but **my own debt from cycle 86**: `seedBootstrapData` gained a `normalized` column while this test fixture's `place_names` schema was never updated. Fixed by mirroring the fuller fixture in the same file. **upsertUrl caused zero test failures.** **(3) Self-inflicted trap caught immediately:** my first fixture comment wrapped *normalized* in BACKTICKS inside a JS template literal, terminating the string so esbuild parsed the SQL as JavaScript (`Expected ")" but found "normalized"`) - the same class as the recorded `crawl-status-client` template trap; rewritten backtick-free with a note in the fixture explaining why. **(4) PERF VERDICT: VOID, on evidence.** Within a SINGLE run at identical config the `db-on-normal` arm gave **82.0 / 50.1 / 49.8 ms/page - a 64% swing between replicates**. That instability cannot resolve the ~2 ms effect, so I did NOT run the OLD half: it would have cost ~10 minutes to produce a number requiring discard. `upsertUrl` stands as correctness-verified (10/10 semantics + full suite green) and perf-unproven. Repo state verified after: no stash entries, both intended files modified, dist in sync with src. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline, sequential diagnosis) | suite run to completion and green; failure attributed to a specific prior change rather than assumed; fixture fix verified in isolation (5/5) then in full suite (918); template-literal breakage caught and fixed before commit; tree state explicitly verified | **The blocker I had escalated to the owner as an open risk - "the shared module's test suite cannot be run" - was a missing `run` flag. I spent a cycle reporting an infrastructure crash that was a watcher waiting patiently for a file to change. Lesson: before escalating a tool as broken, check how it is INVOKED; `npm test` semantics vary per repo and a hang is far more often watch mode than a crash.** Second: the one real failure was mine from nine cycles earlier, surfaced only because I finally ran the suite properly - **a test suite you cannot run is a test suite that hides your own debt**, and the cost compounds silently. Third: I declined to run the OLD half of the comparison. With 64% within-run variance the result would have been noise dressed as a measurement, and "void, here is the variance that makes it void" is a more useful report than a delta I would have to retract next cycle. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 26 / Step 26: FIXED the bug where `src/crawl.js` silently discarded a command-line start URL - verified end-to-end with the very fixture that exposed it (Opus 5).** **Bug:** flags follow "last match wins" but the start URL is a POSITIONAL; the merge was `[...configArgv, ...directArgv]` with `configArgv[0] = config.startUrl`, and the normalizer takes the FIRST positional - so whenever `crawl.js.config.json` set a startUrl, the URL typed on the command line was **silently discarded**. Cycle 18 measured the consequence: a benchmark aimed at a local fixture crawled **https://www.theguardian.com** instead, with no warning, and only the fixture's own request counter reading **0** revealed it. **Fix:** `findExplicitStartUrl(directArgv)` detects a CLI start URL and the merge drops the config's; deliberately conservative - the token must be a bare http(s) URL that is NOT the value of a space-separated flag (`--cached-seed https://...` passes a URL TO a flag), and when ambiguous it declines rather than hijacking the crawl target. The override is now **announced**, not silent. **Verified END-TO-END, not just by unit test:** `Starting crawler for 127.0.0.1` (previously `www.theguardian.com`), fixture **requests=2** (previously **0**), override line printed. **14 new tests pass** (CLI URL wins; config FLAGS still apply; config URL retained when none given; no false override when identical; 4 detector cases incl. the flag-value case it must decline); CLI suite 106 passed. **Pre-existing failure isolated by stash-and-rerun and NOT edited to pass:** `configArgs > returns direct argv when provided` asserts `readFile` is never called, but `resolveCliArguments` always attempts the config load and falls back - the assertion encodes a stale expectation while the behaviour is correct; changing another author's assertion needs their intent. Also confirmed 4 pre-existing stash entries in this repo are from earlier sessions (Dec 2025 / Mar 2026), not orphans of mine. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline, single change + verification chain) | end-to-end verification with the original detecting instrument (fixture request counter 0 -> 2); 14 targeted tests incl. negative cases; pre-existing failure proven pre-existing before being left alone; stash restored in a separate short command and tree verified | **The decisive verification was not the 14 unit tests - it was re-running the exact scenario that first exposed the bug and watching the fixture's counter go 0 -> 2. A unit test proves the merge function behaves; only the end-to-end run proves the CRAWLER now goes where it was told. When a bug was originally found by an instrument, re-use that instrument to prove the fix.** Second: I found a failing test in the file I had just edited and resisted two temptations - assuming I broke it, and editing the assertion so it passes. Stash-and-rerun showed it predates me, and it encodes a stale expectation about someone else's design intent, so it stays. **A failing test you did not cause is information, not a chore.** Third: the detector declines when ambiguous; for a change that redirects where a crawler points, defaulting to the previous behaviour is the right failure mode. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 27 / Step 27: cbc.ca 404s - shape diagnosed decisively, my TWO leading hypotheses both KILLED by evidence, emitting mechanism NOT found. Reported as partial rather than dressed up (Opus 5).** **Shape:** every cbc 404 is a `/en/...` CORPORATE path (ombudsman, media-centre, help-centre, vision/governance, impact-and-accountability, your-public-broadcaster); every 200 is `/news/canada/...`. Recent PK window 200x2,236 vs 404x178. **Those exact paths live on a DIFFERENT host** - `cbc.radio-canada.ca/en/...` - and the DB holds only 26 radio-canada URLs against 84 re-homed `www.cbc.ca/en/...`. **HYPOTHESIS 1 KILLED (mine): the normaliser is not rewriting the host** - `urlPolicy.js:40` is `new URL(rawUrl, this.baseUrl)` and `new URL()` IGNORES the base when the input is absolute, so resolution is correct by construction. **HYPOTHESIS 2 KILLED (mine): the homepage is not the source** - fetched `https://www.cbc.ca/` and counted **7 absolute `cbc.radio-canada.ca/en/` hrefs, 0 root-relative `/en/` hrefs, no `<base>` tag**, so homepage markup cannot yield `www.cbc.ca/en/...`. **Timing:** 83 of 84 such URLs were created today by this session's own crawls, not legacy. **Reframing:** 404s are not a cbc quirk at all - theguardian 2,997 / aljazeera 1,904 / dw 862 / bbc 350 / cnn 324 across the DB; cbc was just the legible instance. Remaining untested candidates named: sitemap ingestion, a non-homepage CBC page using root-relative `/en/`, or URL-synthesis tooling (this repo has guess-place-hubs-style pattern construction, which produces 404s by design). Cheapest next check specified. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline DB archaeology + one polite single-page fetch) | pattern established from status-partitioned samples; cross-host existence check; ONE live page fetch to read the actual markup rather than assume it; creation-date check to rule out legacy data; cross-host 404 census to test whether the item was even cbc-specific | **I went in expecting to find our resolver mangling absolute URLs, and the evidence killed that in two steps - `new URL()` semantics rule it out by construction, and the page itself contains zero root-relative `/en/` hrefs. Fetching the actual page instead of reasoning about what it probably contained is what settled it.** The honest output is a narrowed search space, not a fix, and saying so beats inventing a mechanism that fits. Second lesson: the cross-host 404 census reframed the whole item - cbc's 19% looked like a cbc bug, but Guardian has 2,997 404s; **before optimising an instance, check whether it is an instance of something general**, or you fix the example rather than the class. Third: 83 of 84 URLs were created today, so this is a live behaviour of the current crawler, not archaeology - worth pursuing next cycle rather than filing away. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 28 / Step 28: quantified the 404 class as MODEST (~3% of fetches) and recommended STOPPING rather than fixing it; narrowed the mechanism to one candidate by eliminating three more with evidence; and re-verified my own politeness claims against a scare (Opus 5).** **Measure-first outcome:** today **5.69% of fetches were non-200** (404 **3.16%**, 304 1.14%, 403 0.53%, 402 0.51%, 406 0.35%) over 15,621; across ~183k fetches 200 is **92.67%** and 404 **3.10%**. **Suppressing ALL 404s buys ~3% of fetch budget** - real but far below the 2.88x concurrency win already shipped, so the recommendation is to STOP, not to build. **Mechanism narrowed by elimination:** normaliser rewrite NO (`new URL` ignores base for absolute); homepage markup NO (0 root-relative `/en/`, 7 absolute, no `<base>`); **link extraction NO (`links` has 4,874,880 rows and ZERO point at these URLs)**; **sitemap ingestion NO (`sitemap_cache` 359 rows, ZERO mention them)**; leaving **URL synthesis** as the sole remaining candidate. **Politeness scare investigated and my claim held:** the broad window showed **1,491 x 429**, which appeared to contradict my repeated "zero 429s across every cycle" - by date they are ALL historical (May 2026: 672, Feb: 660, Apr: 151) and **ZERO occurred today**; the cycle-19 claim was correctly scoped to its PK window. **83 x 403 today are NOT from the concurrency change:** nytimes 68 (carrying **863 pre-existing** 403s, a standing paywall block), cbchelp 9, japantimes 3, help.scmp 2, cnn 1 - all paywall/support hosts with prior block history, and the 7 concurrency-3 validation hosts showed zero. Shipped default of 3 stands. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline DB analysis) | class quantified over two windows (today 15,621 and ~183k fetches); each discovery path excluded by a direct count rather than argument; 429 scare resolved by date-partitioning; 403s attributed via pre-existing per-host history | **I went looking for a bug to fix and the measurement said not to bother - 404s are 3.1%, against a 2.88x win already banked. Quantifying the class BEFORE hunting the mechanism is what turned an open-ended hunt into a bounded decision; had I found the synthesis path first I would probably have fixed it and reported a 3% improvement as if it mattered.** Second, and more important: a broad query surfaced 1,491 x 429 and my instinct was that I had been over-claiming safety to the owner. Date-partitioning showed all of them predate today and my scoped claim was accurate - **but the right move was to check rather than to assume my earlier statement was fine**, because the cost of a wrong politeness claim is borne by real publishers. Third: eliminating a hypothesis with a COUNT (0 of 4.87M link rows) is worth more than eliminating it with an argument. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 29 / Step 29: found and fixed a WARM-UP bug in my own instrument that had been mis-attributed to the machine for four cycles, then closed the `upsertUrl` perf question as a NULL - my pre-registered prediction FAILED (Opus 5).** **(1)** The stability gate failed at **55.6%** spread on one arm, but only the FIRST replicate was inflated (**81.3 ms** vs a stable **49-51 ms** across reps 1-3; reps 1-3 alone spread **4.8%**). Cycle 25 saw the identical shape (82.0, then 50.1, 49.8) and I concluded "the machine slowed 2.5x", voiding a measurement that was actually sound. **`postfetch-cost.js` now runs and DISCARDS a warm-up replicate per arm** (cold OS file cache, cold V8, cold SQLite page cache). Gate after the fix: **4.6% / 6.5% / 3.2% - PASSES.** **(2) OLD vs NEW measured in ONE session:** control arm `db-off-normal` (which the change cannot affect) **27.9 → 42.7 ms = 1.53x**; `db-on-normal` **33.2 → 50.8 = 1.53x**; persistence **5.3 → 8.1 = 1.53x**. **Every arm scaled by exactly 1.53 including the control**, so the machine ran 1.53x slower in the NEW half; normalised, persistence is **16% of per-page cost in BOTH**. **VERDICT: NO MEASURABLE DIFFERENCE; the pre-registered -1.5 to -2.5 ms/page prediction FAILED.** **The lesson in the null:** the profile attributed 1.79 ms/page of self-time to `exec`+`prepare`, but removing 4 statements and a per-call `prepare` bought nothing - the cost is the WRITE (I/O + index maintenance), not compiling the SQL. **CPU self-time in a profile is not the same as removable wall time.** Disposition: KEEP the change labelled perf-neutral - bound parameters retire hand-rolled `'` escaping of crawled third-party strings and one cached statement beats five interpolated ones, with correctness 10/10 and the ncdb suite green - but it must never be cited as a speed win. Closes the last open performance claim attached to shipped work. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (offline measurement chain) | stability gate run BEFORE measuring; warm-up fix verified by re-running the gate; OLD and NEW measured in ONE session; control arm read FIRST and it carried the verdict; stash/rebuild/restore/rebuild done as SEPARATE short commands with tree verified (src 3, dist 3, 0 stashes) | **For four cycles I blamed the machine for a defect in my own harness. The tell was there every time - only the FIRST replicate was ever the outlier - and I read a 64% "machine variance" off it instead of noticing the shape. Lesson: when variance is concentrated in the first observation, suspect COLD START, not the environment; a warm-up replicate is one line and would have saved a voided cycle.** Second: the control arm did the entire job. Raw numbers said my change made things 53% SLOWER; the control moved 53% too, so the honest answer was "no difference". **Without an arm the treatment provably cannot touch, I would have reported a large regression that does not exist** - the second time this cycle-run that exact control has prevented a false claim. Third, and the most transferable: a profiler's self-time is not a budget of removable work. 1.79 ms of `exec`/`prepare` frames vanished from the code and the wall clock did not move, because the frames were accounting for I/O the statement still has to do. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 30 / Step 30: CONSOLIDATION - added a top-of-doc summary (§0) so the next session starts from conclusions instead of re-reading 29 append-only sections, with an explicit RETRACTED table (Opus 5).** The fix-queue doc had grown to 1,340 lines of chronological appends in which disproved hypotheses sit alongside confirmed ones with equal visual weight - a reader starting at the bottom would find cycle 20's retracted parse-scaling claim before cycle 21's correction. §0 now carries: **0.1 shipped + evidence** (worker-mode default, stored politeness floor, concurrency 1→3, CLI start-URL fix, upsertUrl labelled PERF-NEUTRAL, the 5-tool instrument suite); **0.2 RETRACTED - do not reuse** (cycle 5-8 throughput figures invalid via mixed-timestamp compare; "+23.1 ms parse scaling" was 5.6x too high; "heavy pages cost twice"; "machine slowed 2.5x" was my own cold-start artifact; "`rpm: 30` imposes a 2 s floor" - the default is inert; queue starvation falsified; the upsertUrl -1.5 to -2.5 ms prediction FAILED); **0.3 cost model**; **0.4 settled questions** (host selection ρ = −0.093, cap not binding, 404s ~3% ⇒ STOP, live measurement futile at 60% variance); **0.5 ranked open items**; **0.6 operating notes that bite** (read the control arm first, `npm test` is watch mode, the DL cap resets on every server, owner gates). | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent (documentation synthesis from this session's own verified records) | every summary line traces to a cycle section with its measurement; retractions listed with their corrected values rather than deleted | **Writing the retraction table was the part worth doing. Seven claims I had stated confidently are now recorded as wrong WITH their corrected values - and in an append-only doc those wrong claims were still sitting there, undated relative to their corrections, ready to be picked up by whoever reads the middle of the file. A summary that only listed wins would have been worse than no summary, because it would have left the disproved material discoverable and unmarked.** Second: consolidation is not overhead at cycle 30 - the doc had become a liability where a reader could act on a retracted number in good faith. **The cost of stale documentation scales with how confident the writing sounds**, and this workstream's sections sound confident throughout, including where they were wrong. | -->

| 2026-07-26 | **CRAWL-PERF cycle 31 / Step 31: ASKED the owner whether 30 self-directed cycles of perf work were still the right focus, then executed their answer - the original 10 sites x 200 pages ask, end-to-end, with ZERO 429s (Opus 5).** After 30 cycles inside a queue whose top levers were closed (concurrency 2.88x shipped, host selection settled at ρ = −0.093, cap shown non-binding, 404s ~3%), the remaining items were each 1-3%, so I put the choice to the owner rather than spend another cycle. Answer: **"Run a real crawl and just use it."** Result: **2,167 pages fetched (2,069 x HTTP 200), 696.1 MB, 495 KB/s average over 24 min with a 649 KB/s peak at minute 15, 429=0, 403=6**, all 10 hosts fetched. **Archive: +2,192 responses, +7,576 URLs discovered, +1,547 articles analysed.** Per host: thehindu 312, npr 298, cbc 289, irishtimes 279, globeandmail 246, aljazeera 216, abc 207, apnews 163, bbc 122, cnn 13. **Explicitly NOT cited as a speedup:** cycle 9 (375 KB/s) and cycle 11 (481 KB/s) used 15 hosts, this used 10, on a different day on a machine that drifts 1.53x - the controlled number stays the fixture's 2.88x. Shape recorded honestly: throughput peaked at 649 KB/s by minute 15 then declined as jobs drained, so the 495 average includes the tail. Incidental for the open 404 item: `cbc.radio-canada.ca` took 6 fetches, so absolute cross-host links ARE followed correctly. | ~1 turn (Opus 5, medium), no subagent | pre-flight asserted jobs=0/workers=0; counts by PK watermark (immune to the mixed timestamp format); 429/403 counted per run; archive delta measured against a baseline captured before the run | **The most useful thing this cycle was asking. Thirty cycles of self-directed work had closed the big levers and left me optimising 1-3% items; the owner's answer redirected effort to validating the whole workstream in the use case that motivated it, which is worth more than another 2%. An agent given a recursive prompt will keep finding next steps indefinitely - noticing that the remaining steps are small and putting the choice back to the owner is part of doing the job well, not an abdication of it.** Second: I had every incentive to present 495 KB/s against the earlier 375 KB/s as a win, and it would have been wrong - different host count, different day, drifting machine. The controlled 2.88x already exists; **a real run demonstrates capability, it does not measure improvement**, and conflating the two would have undone the measurement discipline this workstream was built on. | -->

| 2026-07-27 | **AGI-WORKFLOW cycle: three owner asks delivered as working artifacts — machine-checkable repo SCOPE, capability RESEARCH grounded in 76 measured cycles, and the SVG PROGRESS workflow (owner: most important) rendering the loop's own history (Fable 5).** **(1) Scope:** `config/repo-scope.json` (15 entries; owner-confirmed vs agent-INFERRED distinguished — the three jsgui3 repos are marked consume-only pending an owner ruling; `knownIssue` = loud-WARN-not-fail) + `tools/dev/check-repo-scope.js`, registered in the RB-011 probe manifest so scope is verified at every orient. Born from two real drift incidents: the ecosystem doc said "EMPTY placeholder" months after the engine held 36 files, and a `.git`-based survey silently skipped the one unversioned repo. Stale doc line corrected with a dated note. **(2) Research:** `docs/agi/CAPABILITY_RESEARCH_2026-07-27.md` — per-turn throughput levers with evidence (fixture-over-live was THE multiplier: 60%→5% noise), the recursive-prompt grammar + regeneration rules distilled from 31 hand-regenerated cycles, the substrate principle (emit the machine-readable form + probe alongside any durable claim), Goodhart/schema-drift risks stated up front; RB-013 (delivered), RB-014 (prompt generator, open), RB-015 (SVG v2, open) registered. **(3) SVG workflow SHIPPED:** `tools/agi/progress-svg.js` renders `docs/agi/progress/progress.svg` from the 76 ledger stanzas — 98 verified improvements, 118/132 defects pre-ship (89%), 3 corrections drawn as red diamonds ON the progress line, 16,856 pages. Design: regenerate-never-mutate (modify = edit data via `--annotate`, re-render), deterministic (byte-identical for same inputs; data-through date from the DATA), every tile a recountable count, palette validated (gold #b8862e / green #55a377 / red #b34d4d, worst CVD dE 16.1), 5/5 jest incl. annotation-escaping. Wired into BOOT close-the-cycle; loose-first integration (CLI + committed SVG) with tight layers demonstrated (rendered into chat this turn) and described-not-installed (hooks are owner actions). **Probe suite run end-to-end: repo-scope PASSES; and the ncdb-debt ratchet FAILED at 242 vs 241 — correctly catching MY cycle-102 `storedRateLimitProvider.js` raw SQL.** Owed: delegate that lookup to ncdb. bridge-health fail = bridge not running (~3.8 days), environmental. | ~1 turn (Fable 5), no subagent (build turn; each artifact verified by running it) | 5/5 jest on the SVG tool · real-ledger render sanity-checked (no NaN, 18.4 KB) · scope checker run against disk (0 fail, 1 known-WARN) · probe suite executed proving the registration · palette passed all 4 validator checks after 2 iterations | **The probes caught me. The ncdb-debt ratchet failed at orient because of SQL I added ten cycles ago — which is the system working exactly as designed: a constraint I wrote in a calmer moment out-argued the in-the-moment me who found inline SQL expedient. That is the concrete answer to "how do agents advance capability here": not by being smarter per-turn, but by leaving behind constraints and instruments that make the NEXT agent's carelessness loud.** Second: the owner's SVG directive forced a design question — what should a progress picture make VISIBLE? The honest answer (retractions on the line, counts a tool can recount) is the same discipline as §0.2's retraction table, now in pixels; a wins-only picture would have been prettier and worse. Third: scope went from prose to a probe in one turn because the manifest format (confirmed/inferred, knownIssue) let uncertainty be ENCODED instead of resolved prematurely — the jsgui3 question is now standing, visible, and owner-addressable instead of buried in a chat answer. | -->

| 2026-07-27 | **AGI-WORKFLOW cycle: jsgui3 ruling RECORDED in the scope manifest, and the owner's project-status mini page SHIPPED on the full jsgui3 stack (server + client + html), live in the integrated browser (Fable 5).** **Ruling:** jsgui3-* entries flipped to confirmed:true, consume-only with "fixes and improvements permitted when needed"; jsgui3-server added to the manifest (now actually consumed). **The page** (src/ui/server/projectStatus/: statusData.js + controls.js + server.js, port 3184, launch name `project-status`): renders the same substrate as the SVG - ledger stanzas, repo-scope manifest + the scope checker's checkEntry, annotations - as a status board: 101 verified improvements with a milestone progress bar, stat chips (77 cycles, 89% pre-ship, 3 corrections, 16.9k pages), WORK panel (current focus / follow-ups owed / AWAITING OWNER DECISION - the three standing decisions render there), 14 module cards with condition from disk (the unversioned engine shows "NOT UNDER VERSION CONTROL" in red), milestone badges. **Mid-build owner refinement honoured: game-UI VISUAL design kept (chunky panels, striped gold bar, cards, badges), gaming VOCABULARY stripped** (QUEST/PARTY/XP/LV/"Singularity Wrangler"/"NO SAVE FILE" all replaced with plain engineering terms; verified in-DOM: no gaming-term regex hits). **Real defect found + fixed: client activation silently no-opped** - bundle served, class present, DOM tagged, zero errors. Cause: `Active_HTML_Document` only injects the script tag; activation lives in jsgui3-client (self-activates on window load) and resolves types via `jsgui.controls` - the entry required only jsgui3-html and registered nothing. Fix: environment-selecting require (browser -> jsgui3-client/client, server -> jsgui3-html) + registering the controls under both key casings; proven by an in-DOM marker (`ps-client-active` present after reload). Also: the Browser pane reads the WORKSPACE .claude/launch.json (absolute runtimeArgs), not the repo's - the repo copy is silently ignored. Both gotchas appended to the jsgui3 audit memory. | ~1 turn (Fable 5), no subagent (recipe existed; the one unknown - activation - was diagnosed by reading jsgui3-client, not guessing) | SSR smoke via curl before the pane · DOM-verified: 14 cards, clientActive true, gamingTermsGone true, danger condition correct · screenshot reviewed · statusData standalone-tested before the server existed | **The activation failure was invisible by construction - no error, page looks perfect, the client bundle simply never had the machinery. What caught it was insisting on a VERIFIABLE activation marker (a class added in activate()) rather than trusting a rendered page; "looks right" and "is wired" are different claims, and only the second one survives a reload. Same lesson as maxInFlight in cycle 18: build the instrument that verifies the treatment APPLIED.** Second: the owner's mid-turn refinement ("less gamified - I meant the UI design") cost one rewrite because copy and structure were separate concerns; keeping vocabulary in data/strings rather than baked into layout made the pivot cheap. Third: the launch.json misdirection was diagnosed by reading the ERROR's evidence (it listed the names it COULD see, which identified WHICH file was being read) - the error message named the wrong-file problem if read carefully. | -->

| 2026-07-27 | **AGI-WORKFLOW cycle: OWED DEBT PAID — storedRateLimitProvider's raw SQL delegated into ncdb; the ncdb-debt ratchet is GREEN again at exactly 241 (Fable 5).** The probe that failed at every orient since cycle 121 flagged SQL I added in cycle 102. Delegation: new `legacy-domainRateLimits.ts` in news-crawler-db exporting `selectStoredRateLimitForHost(db, normalizedHost)` — statement-cached via the package's own getCachedStatements pattern, contract kept BYTE-IDENTICAL to the raw SQL it replaces (same columns out, both bare/`www.` key forms matched, strictest row preferred), because the normalized-shape trap is the recorded way these delegations go wrong. **6 new vitest cases** incl. an explicit repoint guard asserting the exact raw column names, and a both-forms case proving the crawl never receives the looser of two stored limits. copilot side: the provider now calls the ncdb export; `resolveSqliteHandle` (adapter-shape logic, not SQL) stays; **zero SQL remains in the file**; the 41 seam/throttle jest tests stayed green THROUGH a real createCrawlerDb adapter — the same tests that once caught this seam being a silent no-op now prove the delegation end-to-end. **Ratchet: 242 → 241 signatures, probe PASS (9 passed / 1 environmental fail / 1 skipped).** Also taught the status page about debt closure: stanzas gain `owed_closed[]`, and statusData retires owed items a later cycle closes — without it, a paid debt would have kept rendering as outstanding, which is the same append-only dishonesty §0.2 exists to prevent. | ~1 turn (Fable 5), no subagent (well-scoped delegation with existing pattern + tests on both sides) | 6/6 ncdb vitest + tsc build + dist export verified · 41/41 copilot jest through a real adapter · debt scan 241 · probe suite re-run GREEN · shape pinned by test against the repoint trap | **The satisfying part is structural: the constraint I wrote (the ratchet), the seam I extracted (the provider), and the tests I left behind all cooperated to make paying this debt a 20-minute mechanical task instead of an archaeology session. Past-me flagged the debt loudly, present-me paid it against pinned contracts, and the probe confirms it — that is the leave-behind-constraints loop actually closing, not just being described.** Also: the owed_closed mechanism matters more than it looks — a progress system that can only ACCUMULATE obligations quietly lies once one is paid; closure has to be a first-class record or the honest display decays into a stale one. | -->

| 2026-07-27 | **AGI-WORKFLOW cycle: status page v2 — live in-place refresh + embedded history, verified by a no-reload marker (Fable 5).** Server side: two routes via the framework's own `server_router.set_route` API — `/api/status` (game-state JSON, no-store) and `/progress.svg` (the committed SVG served from disk each request, so a regenerated picture appears on the next refresh). Client side: key nodes carry `data-ps-*` attributes at compose time and `_apply()` updates them in place — header total, milestone bar width, chips, current focus, owed/recent lists (containers now always exist so empty→populated transitions work), per-module conditions, and a cache-busted history image; REFRESH and a 60 s interval both drive it, with a visible "updated HH:MM:SS" stamp and an honest "refresh failed — server away?" fallback. **`CHIP_DEFS` + label builders are shared between server compose and client apply, so the two renderings cannot drift** — the same one-definition discipline as the SVG's recount-only tiles. HISTORY panel embeds progress.svg (naturalWidth 980 confirmed), unifying both progress artifacts in one page. Also humanized owed-slug hyphens. **Verification: the decisive check was a window marker set before clicking REFRESH that SURVIVED the click (`markerSurvived: true`) — proving in-place update rather than reload — plus apiOk, image loaded, owed rendering "none — all clear", clientActive true, 14 cards.** | ~1 turn (Fable 5), no subagent (bounded increment on a working page; the one unknown — the route API — was answered by reading server.js:286 rather than guessing) | no-reload marker · /api/status fetched from the page itself · img.naturalWidth 980 · stamp updated post-click · screenshot reviewed | **The no-reload marker is the cycle's method note: "the page refreshed" is exactly the kind of claim that looks true under a full reload too, so the test had to distinguish the two mechanisms — a variable that only survives one of them. Same family as maxInFlight and ps-client-active: when two implementations produce the same visible outcome, verify the MECHANISM, not the outcome.** Also: sharing CHIP_DEFS between compose and apply removed the classic SSR/client drift bug BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by review — one definition cannot disagree with itself. | -->

| 2026-07-27 | **AGI-WORKFLOW cycle: RB-014 DELIVERED — the next-turn prompt is now generated from the loop's own substrate, closing the owner's stated development goal of prompt generation (Fable 5).** `tools/agi/next-prompt.js` emits the §3 grammar with the DERIVED/CURATED split explicit: PROGRESS ✅ lines from the last stanzas, FOLLOW-UPS OWED and OWNER DECISIONS from `buildStatus()` (the SAME assembled state the status page serves, owed_closed applied — prompt, page and SVG now share one source and cannot disagree), **METHOD (earned) from the stanzas' `second_order[]` slugs** — newest-first, deduped, capped at 10 — which makes the method list what it always claimed to be: rules that exist only because a recorded mistake produced them; and ▶ candidates parsed from RESEARCH_BACKLOG's open rows, **explicitly labelled `[CURATE: selection is judgment]` rather than pretending the tool chose.** 5/5 jest on the pure parts (backlog filter, method dedup/cap, model, render, empties). Run live: output materially reproduces the hand-written cycle-5 prompt. **Honest deltas stated, not glossed:** result-slug humanization is rougher than hand prose; the open-item filter is a status-text heuristic (RB-011 stays a candidate because its status carries a genuine "Remaining:"); and selection remains human/agent judgment by design. | ~1 turn (Fable 5), no subagent (pure-function tool over existing substrate) | 5/5 jest · live run compared against the hand-written prompt it replaces · single-source reuse verified (buildStatus + parseCycleStanzas imports, no reimplementation) | **The prompt was the last hand-maintained artifact in the loop — the page, the SVG and now the prompt all render from the same stanzas, which means the ritual at cycle close (append row + stanza) is the ONLY act of record-keeping, and everything else is projection. The second_order slugs quietly became the most load-bearing field in the stanza: they were written as ledger color for 30+ cycles and are now the literal source of the METHOD section — a reminder that structured fields written honestly compound into tooling, while prose written honestly compounds into re-reading.** Also: the tool labelling its own limits (`[CURATE:]`) matters — a generator that pretended to pick the next step would convert a judgment into a default nobody examines. | -->

| 2026-07-27 | **RB-015 delivered — the record got a schema probe, the picture got a staleness probe, and the schema probe caught two real defects the minute it first ran (Opus 5, cycle 126).** First, the long-standing "2 malformed stanzas" count was diagnosed as a PARSER false-positive: the regex matched `cycle:{...}` documentation placeholders in row prose (the cycle-47 row literally names the convention) as if they were records — carried in metrics and prompts as fact for cycles. Fixed in `parseCycleStanzas` (placeholder skip), fixture-tested. Then `tools/dev/checks/stanza-schema.check.js` (lenient by design: only `id`+`date` required since early stanzas predate later fields; every optional field type-checked; duplicate-id guard) and `tools/dev/checks/progress-svg-staleness.check.js` (determinism makes staleness an exact BYTE COMPARE against an in-memory re-render; red mid-cycle is expected, red at orient means the ritual was skipped) — both registered as orient probes. **The load-bearing event: stanza-schema's FIRST run flagged two real historical record defects invisible to parse-only checking.** Cycle 58's `defects` entry was a bare string — `d.preship` on a string is `undefined`, so a defect the row prose PROVES was caught preship (live-network-watch, fixed before ship) had been silently counted post-ship since it was written. And the 73b addendum row carried a string id that `sort((a.id||0)-(b.id||0))` cannot order and nothing downstream could chart. Repaired the RECORDS, not the consumers: 58 → typed object `preship:true`; 73b → `73.5` + `id_note` preserving the original. Metric correction: defect split 122/15 → 123/14. | ~1 turn (Opus 5; RB-015 spans a context compaction) | 13/13 jest (8 new validateStanza tests anchored on the two real defect shapes + 5 progress-svg incl. placeholder fixture) · both probes green · `cycle-metrics --check` green with 73.5 · SVG regenerated, staleness byte-identical · full probe run · pane page re-verified post-restart (the running server had the OLD parser cached — restart required to see corrected counts) | **A parse-only check misread documentation as breakage AND hid typed rot — both errors at once, in opposite directions. The probe class justified itself on first execution: writing the validator found real defects that 20+ orient passes over green parse counts never surfaced. "Fix the ledger record, not the consumers" held: both repairs made the record MORE honest (58's preship catch now counted where the prose always said it belonged).** Next: backlog is thin — ask the owner where the next block of cycles goes. |

| 2026-07-27 | **Per-repo activity lanes shipped — first Workflow v3 cycle; the progress picture now shows WHICH repos work lands in, per day, across the whole ecosystem (Opus 5, cycle 127; owner block decision: Workflow v3 over crawl product).** Mechanism chosen for determinism: `tools/agi/repo-activity.js` snapshots git commit-dates per repo-scope.json repo (statuses in/consume-only, window = the stanza era) into a COMMITTED `repo-activity.json`; `renderSvg` gained an optional third input rendering one lane per repo (sequential gold shading by commit count, empty day cells kept — an idle lane is information; consume-only lanes italic and shown only when active, hidden count stated in the footer). Live git state never touches the render, so the staleness probe stays an exact three-committed-inputs byte-compare. **The unversioned `news-crawler-itself` renders as a red dashed warning lane ("no .git — not in the record") — the repo-scope knownIssue is now visible in the picture, not just in probe output.** One preship defect: repo root resolved relative to `config/` instead of the repo root, making EVERY repo (including this one) report "no .git" — the absurd preview result was the catch. One honest caveat rendered into the title: lanes show LANDING date, not work date — git proved cycles 47-120 (ledger-dated 07-21..07-26) landed as bulk catch-up commits, zero commits on those days. | ~1 turn (Opus 5) | 20/20 jest (3 new pure-function tests + lane render test incl. no-git warning lane, hidden-count footer, base-layout byte-compat without a snapshot) · snapshot real: 13 lanes, 13 commits 07-21..07-27 incl. owner's jsgui3 commits 07-25 · staleness probe green with the third input · page embed verified live (980×922 natural, aspect preserved, no page code change — disk-serve pattern) · BOOT + next-prompt ON COMPLETION updated to the two-step ritual | **Snapshot-live-state-into-committed-inputs is the load-bearing pattern: it is what lets a picture that depends on the outside world (git) stay a pure function with an exact staleness check. And the landing-vs-work-date distinction the lanes exposed is itself a workflow finding: the ledger records when work was DONE, git records when it LANDED, and this window they disagree by up to 6 days.** Next v3 candidates: next-prompt v2 · RB-008 compliance meta-tests. | -->

| 2026-07-27 | **RB-008 answered YES with a working instruction-compliance meta-test — the first probe that checks the AGENT rather than the code (Opus 5, cycle 128).** Every other probe verifies artifacts; this one verifies that standing directives were actually FOLLOWED: the close-the-cycle ritual (newest stanza committed at HEAD · record files clean · HEAD level with origin/main · repo-activity snapshot covers the newest stanza) plus an owner-GATED directive an agent could quietly violate while "improving" throughput (crawler `concurrency` default ≤ 3). Pure evaluator + impure state-gatherer so every failure mode is unit-testable. **Empirically grounded rather than speculative — the `--history` audit turned c127's qualitative landing-lag observation into a measurement: 77 of 84 stanzas landed ≥1 day after their ledger date, only 7 same-day, worst 6 days (c46-c50 dated 07-21, landed 07-27).** Cross-checked that this is a real compliance gap and not a history-rewrite artifact: commit dates are spread normally through 07-19, then **zero commits repo-wide for seven days** (07-20..07-26) while cycles 46-120 were being recorded, then everything landed on 07-27 — the day the owner had to ask "please get everything into their repos". The probe would have gone red at the first orient on 07-22. **Verified the checks BITE rather than trusting a green first run:** temporarily raised the real crawler default to 8 → B1 red with the gate message, exit 1; restored byte-identically (`git diff --stat` empty). **The live mechanism test then caught a preship defect in my own check — the exact failure mode the cycle is about.** After appending this row, A1 correctly fired but A2 reported "all record files committed" while the ledger was visibly modified: `git()` trims its output, which eats the leading space of the FIRST `git status --porcelain` line only, so `slice(3)` mangled exactly one path and the check emitted a **false GREEN** — the worst possible failure for a compliance test, since it certifies compliance that did not happen. Fixed by asking git about each record path individually and testing emptiness (no positional parsing), verified in both directions, and guarded by a static tripwire test — whose FIRST version promptly matched the comment explaining the defect rather than the code, i.e. documentation read as breakage: the cycle-126 stanza-placeholder false-positive class recurring in a new guise one cycle later, fixed the same way (strip comments, check the code). Three design rules earned and written into the file: an unreadable gate must FAIL (a regex that stops matching is an unenforced gate, not a pass); mid-cycle red on A1-A3 is the ritual in progress, red at ORIENT means last cycle skipped a step; and "regenerate the next prompt" is listed as UNVERIFIED because it leaves no committed artifact — better to name the hole than imply full coverage. | ~1 turn (Opus 5) | 10/10 new jest (every check's failure mode + both parser shapes) · 45/45 tools/dev suite · live gate-violation test on the real file (red, then byte-identical restore) · `--history` audit cross-checked against raw `git log` date distribution · full probe run green except environmental bridge-health | **A compliance probe that has never been red is theatre, so the cycle's real work was proving each check fires — against the actual file, not fixtures. The deeper finding is that the loop's weakest link was never a tool: it was a step the agent kept deferring, invisibly, for 75 cycles, and the only reason it ever got fixed was the owner noticing. Instrumenting the agent's own compliance is what turns "I follow the ritual" from a claim into a measurement.** Next v3 candidates: next-prompt v2 · session-init/per-turn compliance (RB-008 remainder). | -->
